CHAPTER 7
e Questioning Leader
Will anyone follow a leader who embraces uncertainty?
Why do I choose to lead?
Why are we in business? (And by the way—what business are we really in?)
What should a leader ask—and denitely not ask?
How might we create a culture of inquiry?
Should mission statements be mission questions?
Will anyone follow a leader who embraces uncertainty?
After the rst edition of A More Beautiful Question came out in 2014, I hung
out my shingle as Questionologist (a virtual shingle on a website). And
“patients” began contacting me, from companies, nonprots, government
agencies, and schools.
e leaders of these various organizations were dealing with a shared
problem: ey’d been gobsmacked by change. Because of rapid technological
innovation, and because of shifting attitudes in the workplace, and eventually
because of the COVID-19 pandemic—for all these reasons and more, the
whole idea of what it meant to be a leader seemed to be transforming, rapidly.
“e things we’ve been doing for a hundred years at this company don’t
necessarily work anymore,” one executive conded. She said the company
suddenly needed to rethink long-established policies and procedures. But how
do you preserve the identity of the company while turning it into something new?
And how do you get people within the company, who are used to doing things a
certain way, to question those ways and be open to new approaches? Not only was

<!-- PAGE 141 -->
## Source Page 141
this executive grappling with big questions, but she was also very open and
candid about not having answers.
As I encountered a number of leaders with similar attitudes, it surprised me.
Even though my previous research had shown that inventors, innovators, and
creators were often natural questioners, I had a diﬀerent image in mind of the
classic business leader (perhaps from reading too many media proles of
hotshot chief executives over the years). In my mind’s eye, that traditional
leader had certain immutable characteristics and traits: Strong. Fast-moving.
Charismatic. Condent (maybe even to the point of having a certain swagger).
And above all, decisive: e leader I pictured in my head was able to make
snap judgments and rapid-re decisions, without hesitation. Whatever the
problem, this prototypical leader had the answer. ey just knew, and if they
didn’t know, well, then they could rely on their unfailing “gut instinct.”
But what if that model of leadership no longer made sense in today’s world?
I took this question to a number of people who spend their time studying
leaders, training them, advising them: coaches, management consultants,
former chief executives. What I heard, for the most part, was: Yes, the demands
of leadership are changing, dramatically—with questioning at the center of the
change. And no, leaders, in general, are not well equipped for this change.
“e most important thing leaders must do today is to be the ‘chief
question-asker’ for their organization,” said Dev Patnaik, whose Silicon Valley–
based Jump Associates has consulted with leaders of companies ranging from
Target to Nike to the latest tech start-ups. Patnaik then added this cautionary
note: “e rst thing most leaders need to realize is, they’re really bad at asking
questions.”
at shouldn’t be too surprising: Many were trained or conditioned to think
that if they show doubt, it could be seen as a sign of weakness. Better to be
condent and just trust your business-school fundamentals, your past
experience, and your gut.
But that approach doesn’t hold up in the current “VUCA” environment—a
term used to describe a world of unprecedented “volatility,” “uncertainty,”
“complexity,” and “ambiguity.” In such dynamic conditions, you can’t
necessarily rely on outdated B-school theories or on past experience; it might

<!-- PAGE 142 -->
## Source Page 142
be relevant in the new environment, or might not. Whatever “answers” a leader
could lay claim to yesterday may already be obsolete.
As for “gut instinct?” Most of the current scientic research now tells us that
trusting gut feelings is not a reliable way to make important decisions (it’s more
eﬀective to do thoughtful analysis and weigh multiple options).
And what about that swagger? An uber-condent and commanding
presence can still be an asset, but it can also easily become a liability in a
changing workplace where many employees don’t respond well to overbearing
bosses or the old “command-and-control” style of leadership.
Leaders now need a whole new skill set, as well as a diﬀerent mindset. New
leadership attributes include exibility, open-mindedness, curiosity, humility—
and a willingness to articulate and pursue big, open-ended, exploratory
questions. Today, a leader’s “primary occupation must be to discover the
future,” says Ron Shaich, the founder and former CEO of Panera Bread. And
questioning, he adds, is “the tool for navigating uncharted terrain.”
While exploring new opportunities and possibilities, a questioning leader
can also use inquiry to learn from and motivate employees. As top-down,
commanding styles of management give way to more collaborative ones,
leadership “is more about inuence than control,” said the late David B.
Peterson, who had been director of executive coaching and leadership at
Google. Leaders can no longer just order the troops to charge up that hill—
they must rally, coach, and otherwise support people in the eﬀort.
rough eﬀective questioning, a leader can identify challenges that people in
the organization may be struggling with, while also eliciting valuable
information and knowledge from the frontlines and bringing it to the
executive suite. And if the leader is able to establish a culture of inquiry, it can
encourage the kind of company-wide independent thinking and collaboration
that is critical to success in the VUCA world.
THIS IS NOT to suggest this new model of leadership is standard practice at
present. On the contrary, the old top-down, command-and-control model still
holds sway in many organizations. And the problems associated with that old
approach are evident. More than 80 percent of those surveyed by the World
Economic Forum believe we are in the midst of a leadership crisis.

<!-- PAGE 143 -->
## Source Page 143
at crisis manifests itself in scandals among chief executives and politicians,
in failing schools, in corporate malfeasance and government shutdowns, and in
ongoing failures to prevent (or even adequately respond to) problems ranging
from humanitarian crises to sexual harassment in the workplace. In the words
of Deborah Ancona, director of the MIT Leadership Center, “e recent past
has showcased a leadership stage featuring Greek tragedies lled with leaders
who are toxic and corrupt, out of touch and unable to act.”
Interestingly, this failure of leadership has come at a time when there is more
available information on leadership than ever before—a veritable glut of
leadership advice, dispensed in hundreds of thousands of articles, books, and
blogs on the subject.
Given that there are so many answers to the questions of “how to be a
leader” at our ngertips, why aren’t we seeing better results? Douglas Conant, a
former Campbell Soup Company chief executive who now heads up
Northwestern University’s Kellogg Executive Leadership Institute, explains that
becoming a better leader is an “inside out” process—one that begins with self-
questioning.
is means that all of those leadership tips from blog posts or TED talks
won’t help much if you haven’t rst claried why you want to lead, what
matters to you above all else, and how you might develop and articulate your
own philosophy and strategies. is hard work can help form a solid
foundation of values and approaches that will support the leader as they begin
to actually do the work.
But most leaders don’t do that intellectual groundwork, Doug Conant says.
Many ascend without thinking about what awaits them at the top. ey rise by
way of some combination of productivity, ambition, and deft maneuvering.
Our leaders often become leaders simply because they are able to “climb the
greasy pole of whatever hierarchy they decide to attach themselves to,” notes
author William Deresiewicz in his elegant writing on leadership.
Having arrived at a position of power—and being unsure what to do next—
they begin to practice what Conant calls “seat of the pants leadership.” ey
may take training courses or speed-read through some of that vast leadership
literature, but the “outside” advice doesn’t stick because they haven’t done the
“inside” work.

<!-- PAGE 144 -->
## Source Page 144
Seat-of-the-pants leadership has always been a problem, but it is even more
so today. e pressures of the VUCA environment cause crises to develop more
quickly, and if leaders aren’t prepared—because they haven’t given enough
advance thought to the challenges—they can quickly compound the problems.
“ey end up cutting corners to make shareholders happy, or spinning half-
truths to temporarily placate employees,” Conant says, until the whole short-
term house of cards comes tumbling down.
So how does a leader, or one who aspires to lead, avoid this trap? Conant
and other leadership experts advise beginning with a few simple yet critical
questions.
Why do I choose to lead?
It’s common for aspiring leaders to be focused on what they hope to get from
being a leader (status, glory, money) without considering whether there is a
greater purpose driving their leadership aspirations—or clarifying what that
purpose might be. e demands of leadership now are so great that unless one
is fueled by a sense of purpose that transcends personal ambition—and unless
one enjoys the actual day-to-day work of engaging with and leading others—
the pursuit may prove unsatisfying or unsustainable in the long run.
Susan Cain, author of the books Quiet and Bittersweet, observed in a New
York Times essay that universities, and the students entering them, have
become hyper-focused on leadership. (e schools want to be seen as
institutions that produce leaders, while the students all want to become
leaders.) But as Cain points out, both sides seem to be dening leadership in
shallow terms—based on how many achievements a student can list or the
number of clubs that student has become president of. is creates the sense
that the overall objective is “to be a leader for the sake of being in charge,
rather than in the name of a cause or idea [the student cares] about deeply.”
Noting that the world needs leaders who are called to service rather than to
status, Cain oﬀered up a beautiful question: What if we said to our would-be
leaders, “Take this role only if you care desperately about the issue at hand”?
To reframe Cain’s challenge as a “self-question,” anyone considering a
leadership role in any organization might want to rst ask, 1) Why do I want to

<!-- PAGE 145 -->
## Source Page 145
lead this endeavor or these people? And 2) Why would they want me to lead them?
If you have a worthwhile answer for the rst question, it may apply to the
second, as well. For example, a school principal might answer that rst
question by saying they want to lead the school because they have a clear vision
for how to better inspire students and support the teachers.
at also happens to be a very good answer to the second question—
because who wouldn’t want a leader with that kind of vision? But imagine if the
principal’s answer to that rst question was more about self; as in, I feel I’m
entitled after all these years to be the principal of this school. at would be a bad
answer to the second question.
In considering the question of Why do I choose to lead?, it’s worth keeping in
mind that leadership today is, increasingly, a contact sport. As the command-
and-control approach from a remote corner oﬃce gives way to more of a
hands-on, on-the-ground approach, the job calls for “softer” skills: listening,
eﬀective communication, coaching. And it demands another quality that can
be in short supply in many leadership circles: humility.
In the past, there has been “a natural tendency to elect self-centered,
overcondent and narcissistic individuals as leaders,” observes the renowned
organizational psychologist Tomas Chamorro-Premuzic, explaining that we
“commonly misinterpret displays of condence as a sign of competence.” (And
men are usually the beneciaries of this error, wrote Chamorro-Premuzic in an
essay headlined “Why Do So Many Incompetent Men Become Leaders?”)
Overcondence breeds hubris, which can then infect an organization’s
culture. A fascinating study of the problem of “executive hubris” found that
the trouble often begins with leaders who exude condence (as they are
expected and even trained to do)—and then manifests in other, more harmful
behaviors by the leader, including a tendency to micromanage, to blame others
when things go wrong, to treat disagreement as a personal slight, to out rules,
and to engage in self-glorication.
Such behavior by leaders has never been desirable, but today it’s an almost
surere recipe for failure—because it tends to work against the increasingly
critical needs for collaboration, innovation, and employee
engagement/retention.

<!-- PAGE 146 -->
## Source Page 146
Nevertheless, leaders still must “exude condence” in order to instill faith in
followers. So the balancing act for today’s leaders is to be condent yet
humble: willing to question one’s own judgments, to defer to the needs of
others, and share credit, while also projecting a sense of authority, boldness,
and a belief in oneself. As Conant puts it: “I must be willing to admit I may
not have the answer . . .  but at the same time be condent I can help all of us
to nd the answers.”
When Jim Hackett, former CEO of the design and furniture manufacturing
company Steelcase, rst took the helm at that company, he struggled with
what his role should be as a leader, wondering, What does a CEO look like and
feel like?
Hackett initially focused on some of the wrong questions. He was overly
concerned with what others (in particular, the family that owned the company)
wanted or expected from him. But gradually, as he lived with his beautiful
question about his role, he concluded that a leader’s primary job is this: to
project a clear and distinctive point of view about where the organization is headed
and how to get there. at clear vision is arrived at, and constantly modied and
sharpened, through deep reection and questioning.
Hackett told me he believes that deep thinking is a lost art in today’s
business environment. “ere is an over-celebration of getting things done,” he
said. “For a long time, I have been asking myself this question: Where did the
balance between thinking and doing get out of equilibrium?
What is your tennis ball?
Drew Houston, founder of the online storage service Dropbox, thinks all would-be entrepreneurs should try to answer the above question. “The
most successful people are obsessed with solving an important problem, something that matters to them,” according to Houston. “They remind
me of a dog chasing a tennis ball.” To enhance your prospects, “ﬁnd your tennis ball—the thing that pulls you.” PayPal cofounder Peter Thiel
believes entrepreneurs can ﬁnd ideas to pursue by asking themselves, What is something I believe that nearly no one agrees with me on? If self-
examination doesn’t work, try looking around: Brian Spaly, a serial entrepreneur in the apparel industry, advises, “Whenever you encounter a
service or customer experience that frustrates you, ask, Is this a problem I could solve?” Lastly, don’t just focus on the mercenary question Will
consumers pay for this? The start-up business coach Dave Kashen thinks the better question to ask about any new venture is, Will this make
people’s lives meaningfully better?
At Steelcase, Hackett tried to emphasize—and even taught courses on—the
importance of doing critical thinking and questioning before taking action.

<!-- PAGE 147 -->
## Source Page 147
“We have to train ourselves to ask questions,” he says. “We have to discipline
ourselves to do it.”
But what should you ask? For those inclined to question, the diﬃculty may
be in guring out the questions that are most critical for a particular
organization to consider, given current challenges and market conditions.
While those key questions vary depending on the individual business, a good
place to start is at the most fundamental level—with questions of purpose.
Why are we in business? (And by the way—what business are we
really in?)
Almost every company would acknowledge that it is in business to make
money so that it can stay in business. But most companies, if you trace their
origins, were started for more complex reasons than that. Many of the
companies featured in this book—Nike, Airbnb, Panera, Netix—started out
on a quest to ll an unmet need, to make some aspect of our lives a bit easier,
more convenient, more enjoyable. Most good companies are born trying to
answer a question and solve a problem, which provides an early sense of
purpose.
But that motivating principle gets buried over time. Asking Why questions
can help to unearth that buried principle. (And if, after being dug up, that
sense of purpose needs to be revitalized, freshened up, and made relevant
again, questioning can help with that, too.)
Sometimes it takes an outsider to point out the basic questions that a leader
should be asking. e legendary management consultant Peter Drucker was
known for this: Drucker once remarked that his greatest strength was “to be
ignorant and ask a few questions.” Often those questions were deceptively
simple, as in Who is your customer? What business are you in?
In trying to answer fundamental questions about its identity and purpose, a
furniture retailer, for example, might decide its purpose is to sell people
furniture. But it could also think of its higher purpose as bringing a sense of
style into the lives of those on a budget; or enabling people to express their
creativity through home furnishings. Getting this right is subtle; advertising
sometimes attaches generic or articial purposes to companies. But if the

<!-- PAGE 148 -->
## Source Page 148
leaders of a company think deeply enough, and question well enough, about
where the company came from, what it does best, and who it serves, they will
often uncover a more meaningful and authentic purpose in the company’s
origins.
To gure out those internal values, the management consultant Keith
Yamashita urges company leaders to look back in time and consider this
question: Who have we (as a company) historically been when we’ve been at our
best?
At the nest moments in a company’s history, Yamashita holds, its core
values usually came shining through. But from time to time it may be
necessary to revisit that past to reaﬃrm the company’s higher purpose, and to
continually ask, What is true about us, at our core?
Knowing that becomes especially critical when a company nds itself in the
midst of dramatic change. e digital revolution has forced many
organizations to rebuild and re-strategize, sometimes pushing them into
unfamiliar territory. A company that has gured out the basic questions of
identity and purpose is in a better position to handle unsettling new questions
such as What business are we in now?
Some years back, leaders at Nike observed the profound changes that digital
technology was having on athletes such as runners. ey were fumbling with
various gadgets—heart monitors, music players—as they ran. Nike went into
classic Why mode (Why does this problem exist? Why hasn’t anyone addressed it?).
en, in considering What If possibilities, the idea emerged of creating a
hybrid, networked tool, connected to a Nike running shoe, that could
encompass many of a runner’s needs: from measuring distances, to charting
progress, to getting pumped up by music, to connecting with other runners.
But getting to How was another matter; Nike was a sneaker company, not a
digital-device maker. Nike’s chief executive, Mark Parker, ended up striking a
collaborative deal with Steve Jobs and Apple (not easy: Jobs initially told Parker
he should stick to his sneakers). But eventually, the two companies
collaborated on a hybrid product, Nike+, which wirelessly connected a Nike
running shoe to an Apple iPod device, which was in turn connected to a
website—enabling a runner to program music, track running and health data,

<!-- PAGE 149 -->
## Source Page 149
communicate with other runners, and nd running partners, among other
needs.
For Nike, the new product helped them begin to think outside the shoebox.
Nike went on to introduce a line of technology and communication products,
gradually becoming as much a tech company as a shoe company. So if you ask,
What business is Nike really in?, the answer is constantly changing—though it’s
grounded in the core purpose of serving an athlete’s lifestyle needs, in whatever
form they might take.
Nike isn’t the only company going through these kinds of transformational
changes of late. A number of today’s leading companies—Apple, Netix,
Amazon—have increasingly found success by moving outside their original
area of expertise. In the VUCA marketplace, whatever got you to where you
are today might not be the thing that gets you to the next level. And today’s
leader must be exible enough to navigate these dramatic shifts while
maintaining that core identity: How can we change what we do without
compromising who we are?
What should a leader ask—and deﬁnitely not ask?
Once you’ve explored fundamental questions about why you want to lead and
why your organization exists, there are countless directions a leader’s questions
might move in—questions about strategy or business philosophy, hypothetical
questions about the future, practical ones about the here-and-now. e
questions that will emerge as central ones for an organization will tend to
diﬀer, depending on the situation and the particular vision of a leader.
But since this book is about “beautiful questions”—ones that tend to rise
above the rest and can serve to shift your thinking or guide your actions—I
thought it could be useful to inquire among top leaders and leadership experts
about the questions that have had a truly galvanizing eﬀect on them.
Here are six to consider:
1. How can we become the company that would put us out of business?
“Managers watch the bottom line, but leaders keep their eyes on the
horizon,” declared the leadership guru Warren Bennis. Today’s leaders

<!-- PAGE 150 -->
## Source Page 150
have to be futurists, anticipating changes not yet on the horizon
because they may still be years away from happening. And one of the
most important things to try to anticipate is a potential threat that
does not yet exist.
Speculative inquiry can be used to do this. e New York
restaurateur Danny Meyer likes to ask this question, which invites you
to envision a future deadly rival. Essentially: What might this predator
look like, and why would it have an advantage over us? Having gured
that out, you can think about how to make yourself more like that
future predator (or at least develop defenses against it).
2. How might we be a cause and not just a company? Today, discerning
consumers and talented employees are drawn to brands and companies
that actually stand for something, beyond just selling products. e
consultant Tim Ogilvie of Peer Insight suggests this question as a way
to think about becoming fully committed to a worthwhile purpose—
which can motivate employees and forge a greater bond with
customers.
But notice the wording of the question—which focuses on “being” a
cause, not just donating to one. It demands a consistent, ongoing
commitment (expressed in corporate behaviors and policies, as well as
contributions) to a worthy idea or endeavor. Ideally, that cause should
be one that the organization is in a unique position to lead and
support, so that the connection feels natural—e.g., the food company
that nds ways to feed the hungry. e leader should take stock of the
organization’s particular strengths and core values, then match that to
an appropriate need in the world.
3. What would an outsider do? Early in its history, the microprocessor
company Intel found itself facing a diﬃcult decision. e company
had started out making computer memory chips, but as the memory-
chip business began to slow down, Intel’s cofounders, Andrew Grove
and Gordon Moore, had to decide whether to shift the company’s
focus into more promising areas. Yet they were torn: Chips were central
to their identity—and Intel wouldn’t have gotten where it was without
them.

<!-- PAGE 151 -->
## Source Page 151
en Grove posed an interesting question to his partner: If we were
kicked out of the company, what do you think the new CEO would do?
Grove and Moore reasoned that a new leader would feel no emotional
attachment to the declining memory-chip business and would
probably leave it behind. So they did likewise, shifting Intel’s focus to
microprocessors—which set the stage for remarkable growth in the
years to follow.
When companies are facing disruptive change (and these days, what
company isn’t?), old habits and traditions can sometimes get in the way
of progress. And at all times, leaders are subject to their own biases and
habitual thinking. One way to try to get around that is to adopt the
perspective of an outsider.
Why can’t everyone accept credit cards?
Jack Dorsey, one of the cofounders of Twitter, is a business leader who embraces the credo “Question everything.” Upon learning that a friend,
glassworks designer Jim McKelvey, lost a $2,000 sale because he couldn’t accept a potential customer’s credit card, Dorsey wondered, Why is it
that only companies are able to accept credit cards? Partnering with McKelvey, Dorsey envisioned an easy-to-use alternative to clunky, expensive
card-reading equipment: What if all you needed to swipe a credit card was a smartphone or tablet? As to the “how” of making this feasible, Dorsey
and the designers at his start-up, Square, devised a small plastic plug (easily inserted into a smartphone jack) that serves as a card reader, and
added a clean, intuitive user interface, accessible via a smartphone app. The elegant simplicity of Square (and that of Dorsey’s earlier creation,
Twitter) is a product of rigorous inquiry: Dorsey maintains that good design is about removing unnecessary features by continually asking, Do we
really need this? and What can we take away?
4. What should we stop doing? In many organizations, existing projects
and processes build up over time; leaders are often inclined to add on
the new without subtracting the old. e management guru Peter
Drucker felt that to battle this kind of accretion, a leader needed to
regularly ask, What should we stop doing? Drucker referred to this
winnowing practice as “systematic abandonment,” and believed it was
critical because it kept companies from being stretched too thin and
helped to identify and weed out policies that don’t work anymore.
When trying to gure out which policies or practices to abandon,
ask the people who must contend with those policies: employees. e
business consultant Lisa Bodell recommends giving people in the
company the power to decide: What stupid rule would you most like to

<!-- PAGE 152 -->
## Source Page 152
kill? But be sure to provide some guardrails, Bodell says; some rules
may be absolutely necessary and it might even be illegal to get rid of
them.
5. How can we make a better experiment? is question is somewhat
counterintuitive for most company leaders—who tend to think in
terms of oﬀering products or services, not “making experiments.” But
Eric Ries, pioneer of the highly inuential Lean Startup business
movement, points out that the best way for any organization to bring
new ideas into the world is to develop a reliable system for testing and
rening those ideas.
So how do organizations get better at experimenting? Start by
abandoning the notion that you must always bring perfect, nished
products and/or services into the world; instead, develop new ideas in
limited stages, with the emphasis on learning and improving at each
stage. With that in mind, “you work backwards to the simplest possible
thing—the minimum viable product—that can be tested and get you
the learning,” Ries says.
Just this one change—before you get to any of the more complex
Lean Startup methodology—can help unlock the creativity that already
exists in an organization. “Most companies are full of ideas, but they
don’t know how to go about nding out if those ideas work,” Ries says.
“If you want to harvest those ideas, allow employees to experiment
more—so they can nd out the answers to their questions themselves.”
6. Where is my tortoise enclosure? is oﬀbeat question is inspired by the
former Monty Python member John Cleese, also known for his
lectures on creativity. Cleese has talked about the need to nd one’s
own “tortoise enclosure”—that sheltered, quiet place where you can
retreat for extended periods to escape from distraction in order to think
without interruption.
is is critical for leaders, who tend to have endless demands on
their time—decisions that must be made now, constant meetings, and
calls that can’t wait. Amid all of those pressures, the leader must
somehow carve out a time to slow down and think. e investment
guru Warren Buﬀett said, “I insist on a lot of time being spent, almost

<!-- PAGE 153 -->
## Source Page 153
every day, just to sit and think.” Buﬀett’s business partner, Charlie
Munger, conrmed that there are days on Buﬀett’s schedule marked
simply “haircut day,” with nothing else scheduled. On those days,
Buﬀett gets his hair cut—and leaves the rest of the day clear for
thinking.
ere’s a good reason why successful leaders like Buﬀett do this.
According to research by the Boston Consulting Group, “Reection
leads to better insights into innovation, strategy, and execution.” For
leaders who spend much of their time putting out res, it is critical,
BCG maintains, to nd time to stop doing that in order to allow the
mind to work in a diﬀerent way—connecting thoughts, searching for
meaning, trying to get at underlying issues, envisioning future
possibilities.
“When leaders say they don’t have time to think, that’s BS,” says
Doug Conant. “Get up an hour earlier if you must.” Conant’s tortoise
enclosure is in his garden—where he follows an early morning ritual of
reecting on critical questions while having coﬀee. “You can always
engineer the time somehow, but you have to have discipline about
protecting that time,” he adds.
What if women made the rst move?
Whitney Wolfe Herd found that she had unsatisfying experiences with online dating services (she’d previously worked on the dating app Tinder).
With this in mind, Herd started with a simple diagnostic question: What’s broken in online dating? She felt the current system just wasn’t working
for women, who had little control over online interactions with men and sometimes ended up getting harassed. This led to Herd’s ambitious
follow-up question: What if only women could initiate the conversation? She then designed the dating app Bumble so that, with any potential
match, women always got to make the ﬁrst move. Today, Bumble is one of the most popular dating services in the world. Herd’s two-step
questioning approach is classic: Start by inquiring about a large, systemic problem; then proceed to a bold, imaginative What If question that
proposes a possible solution.
As I gathered favorite beautiful questions used by leaders, I also inquired as
to whether there are questions a leader should avoid asking. Here are a few that
stand out as inauthentic, negative questions that are commonly asked; they’re
really more like criticism or complaints posed in the form of a question.

<!-- PAGE 154 -->
## Source Page 154
1. What’s the problem? Leaders often can nd themselves asking this
question or some variation of it (What’s going wrong?, Why isn’t this
working?, etc.). ese types of problem-based questions are,
unfortunately, the starting point of most business discussions and
meetings, says David Cooperrider, a pioneer of “appreciative inquiry,”
which posits that questions focusing on strengths and using positive
language are far more useful to organizations than questions with a
negative focus. Cooperrider maintains that if a leader asks questions
focused on problems and weaknesses, then the organization overall will
tend to be xated on that—rather than focusing on opportunities.
Better to use questions geared to leveraging strengths and achieving
goals. What are we doing well and how might we build on that? What is
the ideal outcome and how do we get closer to that?
2. Whose fault is it? is question focuses attention on nding a
scapegoat when in reality, there is usually plenty of blame to go around
for any failure or problem. A better question is, How can we work
together to shore up any weaknesses? at identies weak links and areas
in need of improvement without focusing too much on blame.
3. Haven’t we tried this already? Also sometimes asked as Why do you
think this would work when it hasn’t worked before? It’s not that a leader
shouldn’t raise questions about proposed strategies—especially if
something similar has been tried previously—but tone is important.
is version of the question comes oﬀ as condescending and even
defeatist. It seems to suggest that everything has been thought of
already, and that because something was tried once and didn’t work, it
should never be considered again. is fails to recognize that some
ideas may have come up short in the past because of bad timing or
poor execution, not necessarily because the idea itself was wrong.
Better to ask, If we tried this now, what might be diﬀerent this time—and
how might that change the results?
How might we create a culture of inquiry?

<!-- PAGE 155 -->
## Source Page 155
So far, we’ve considered ways a leader can begin to ask more and better
questions. But how might a leader encourage others, throughout an
organization, to inquire more?
As is often the case, it’s better to start by rst considering Why before How.
Why would a leader want to encourage more questioning throughout the company?
And is there a case for not doing it?
It’s almost fashionable now for company leaders to talk about the
importance of having a more curious and inquisitive culture, with the
assumption that it’s something every business should try to foster. But surely
there are leaders who are more comfortable in a top-down environment where
people don’t ask questions about policies and practices.
As someone who advocates for more questioning, I sometimes ask leaders, If
your people start asking more questions, what will you actually do with those
questions? Questions shouldn’t be ignored; that just upsets the questioner,
which is the opposite of what fostering questioning is supposed to do. Another
consideration: What if you don’t like the questions your employees are asking?
If a leader is ummoxed by those two questions, it indicates they haven’t
thought through the ramications of encouraging a questioning culture. And if
a leader is fond of saying to their employees, “Don’t bring me questions; bring
me answers!” (or, similarly, “Don’t bring me problems; bring me solutions”), it
may indicate a low tolerance for dealing with unexpected questions,
uncomfortable debate, and uncertainty in general.
So why open the lid and release a potential torrent of employee questions?
In order to innovate, many companies today need new ideas from as many
sources as possible. Questions asked by people at all levels—a manager
reviewing procedures and processes, a worker on the frontlines who detects
ineﬃciencies—can lead to important changes and improvements. Socrates
once said, “All of us are smarter than any of us.” Companies become smarter
and more productive by tapping into the collective intelligence of the group.
A second, related reason to encourage widespread questioning is that
questioning is a proven tool for navigating change—and every organization is
having to deal with rapid and constant change these days. People within those
changing companies can better adapt, and survive, if they’re able to inquire and
learn on the job.

<!-- PAGE 156 -->
## Source Page 156
Moreover, good leaders want their employees to feel content and fullled, if
for no other reason than that it lowers the turnover rate. One of the ways
people feel fullled in their work is by learning; in fact, research suggests many
people are apt to leave their jobs if and when they stop learning. If you want
people within an organization to keep learning, you must give them the
freedom to explore, wonder, and inquire.
So those are the Whys behind leaders encouraging questioning in their
organizations. And they are compelling, particularly for organizations that are
focused on meeting future unknown challenges and also want people
throughout the organization to contribute, be creative, and feel fullled.
IN TERMS OF how to foster a culture of inquiry, one thing seems clear: It
must start at the top. Research on curiosity shows that it tends to ourish best
in an environment where questioning is modeled and encouraged—whether
by a teacher in a classroom, a parent in a home, or a chief executive of a
company.
During the period he was chief executive at Campbell Soup, Doug Conant
eﬀectively modeled questioning by walking the oor at the company and
asking questions at many stops along the way. ink of this as “ambulatory
inquiry.” is on-the-ground, close-contact approach can be a tough sell to
busy executives, many of whom “brush these interactions aside because they’re
too busy trying to get the ‘real work’ done,” Conant says. “But this is the real
work.”
What should be asked at those stops? Opt for open-ended questions, asked
with genuine interest and curiosity behind them. e late Edgar Schein, a
former MIT Sloan School organizational development expert and author of
the book Humble Inquiry, urged leaders to avoid “leading questions, rhetorical
questions, embarrassing questions, or statements in the form of questions.”
As a questioning leader engages directly with employees, it doesn’t just help
those employees—it also provides the leader with critical information needed
to run the organization. Schein observed that a problem plaguing most
organizations is poor upward communication. “Subordinates know lots of
things that would make the workplace better or safer—that they for various
reasons withhold.” e only way to change this, Schein said, is for the leader to

<!-- PAGE 157 -->
## Source Page 157
go directly to subordinates and say, “I’m really interested and I’m listening.”
Until more leaders do that, he added, we will “continue to have accidents and
low quality products because the information isn’t surfacing.”
Indeed, a major part of the purpose of ambulatory inquiry is to identify
problems out there that should be addressed. But don’t ask about
“problems”—better to inquire about “challenges.” You can inquire about them
in a general way (What are some of the biggest challenges you face in your role
within this organization?) or in a more specic way (On this project, what is the
single toughest challenge?). Either type of question enables you to identify areas
of need and provides a nice setup for what Conant always used as his “closing”
question: How can I help?
When not walking the halls, a questioning leader should look for other ways
to integrate questioning into everyday business practices. What if every meeting
began with a question? What if the company held regularly scheduled “question
days” (either in person or on Zoom)—during which groups come together to ask,
“What assumptions can we challenge today?” What if you ask employees to come up
with one ambitious question a week, to be shared with their colleagues?
MODELING QUESTIONING IS only a start: To get employees to follow
suit, the behavior should be rewarded. “It’s the systems and the incentives you
create for people that promote the behavior,” says the Lean Startup’s Eric Ries.
He points out that at most companies, “the resources ow to the person with
the most condent plan, or the person with no failures on their record.” Ries
says companies should direct more budgetary resources to those who are
exploring unanswered questions and conducting promising experiments.
It’s also important for leaders to be on the lookout for ways in which
questioning gets punished—though the punishment may not be obvious or
intentional. For example, an employee might inquire about a problem at their
workplace—say, something the company is not doing as well as it might—and
then be told that since they found the problem, they should try to x it. (In
addition to their normal duties, of course.) Do that to employees and they will
stop pointing out problems and asking questions—because most are not
seeking to add to their workload.

<!-- PAGE 158 -->
## Source Page 158
e better approach is to ask the problem-nders if, and to what extent,
they might want to be involved in working on that problem—and to oﬀer help
so they don’t have to tackle it alone.
e goal in business is not only for people to ask more questions, but for
them to be asking productive—or beautiful—questions that help identify
problems or opportunities, questions that inquire about new ideas and
potential improvements. To that end, organizations should train people on
questioning techniques, methods, and practices. is can run the gamut from
teaching them how to brainstorm in questions (see the Question Formulation
Technique, in chapter 3); how to strengthen critical thinking skills (chapter 8);
how to use questions to solve problems (chapters 4 through 6); and how to ask
questions of other people in eﬀective, non-confrontational ways (chapter 9).
To help people be more productive in their questioning, show them that
questioning can be aimed at achieving a desired outcome. In business, the
point is not to debate philosophical questions endlessly, nor to wonder just for
the sake of wondering (those are ne things to do on one’s own time, of
course). e point is to use questioning to drive a process that can lead to
tangible results. To that end, people should understand the questioning
methods that enable an innovator to steadily progress from wondering about a
new possibility to eventually guring out how to make it a reality—moving
from Why to What If to How.
One more point on fostering a productive culture of inquiry: If you want it
to be truly inclusive and widespread—as opposed to having, say, privileged
pockets of inquiry—start by recognizing that everyone is, or has the potential to
be, a curious questioner. However, it’s also likely that some will be more
reticent than others to openly express their curiosity. In my research, I was
surprised to learn that questioning can be intertwined with issues involving
power and privilege. Some people—whether it’s because they were raised in a
certain type of household or attended Montessori schools—seem to feel more
inclined and maybe even entitled to publicly ask questions. Others—who may
be in more of an outsider position, or perhaps are just more polite or reserved
—might tend to hold back their questions.
e leader’s job is to invite all of those people to share. Ask yourself, Whose
voice might I have missed hearing—and how might I amplify this voice?

<!-- PAGE 159 -->
## Source Page 159
According to leadership advisers Jane Hyun and Audrey Lee, that question can
help identify people in the organization who may be “unheard” based on
ethnic group or gender, but also other factors—such as how long they’ve been
with the company or which department they work in.
In a culture of inquiry, none of those boundaries should matter—because
great questions can and should come from everyone.
Should mission statements be mission questions?
Here’s a nal thought, which I have been sharing with leaders I’ve met in
recent years: If you want to demonstrate to people in your organization that
you are a questioning leader who is trying to foster a culture of inquiry,
consider starting with something highly visible: your company’s mission
statement.
e philosopher Bertrand Russell once said, “In all aﬀairs it’s a healthy thing
now and then to hang a question mark on the thing you have long taken for
granted.” e typical corporate mission statement is something that is often
taken for granted—and occasionally ridiculed. But what if we were to take that
typical mission statement and hang a question mark on the end of it?
As I mentioned above, it’s a way to make a visible gesture in support of
questioning. But it also makes sense in other ways. It’s often assumed that a
declarative “statement” makes a company seem condent—more sure of its
mission, more determined. But mission statements can have a diﬀerent eﬀect
on both customers and employees. ey tend to consist of broad claims that
can come across as arrogant and not entirely credible. Often they’re banal
(Walmart: We save people money so they can live better) or debatable (Yahoo! is the
premier digital media company). ese stiﬀ corporate pronouncements don’t
oﬀer much help in trying to gauge whether a company is actually living up to a
larger goal or purpose.
And sometimes they seem to be proclaiming that the mission has already
been accomplished, implying that the company is now in maintenance mode.
Moreover, mission statements can seem like they’re from another time—a
time when companies acted as if they had all the answers. But in these
dynamic, uncertain times, wouldn’t it be more appropriate to take that static

<!-- PAGE 160 -->
## Source Page 160
statement and transform it into a more open-ended, uid mission question? A
mission question could still be ambitious without being quite so arrogant. For
example: “We make the world a better place through robotics!” gets opened up
by How might we make the world a better place through robotics?
By articulating the company mission as a question, it tells the outside world,
“is is what we’re striving for—we know we’re not there yet, but we’re on the
journey.” It acknowledges room for possibility, change, and adaptability.
Perhaps most important, a mission question invites participation and
collaboration. Questions, by their very nature, challenge people and invite
them to engage with an idea or an issue—and could therefore do likewise in
terms engaging employees with a company mission. Indeed, thinking of a
company mission as a shared endeavor—an ongoing attempt to answer a big,
bold question through collaborative inquiry—seems vastly preferable to having
to live up to a dictum handed down from on high.
Whether or not the mission statement is phrased as a question, a company’s
mission should always be subject to constant questioning:
Does our mission still make sense today?
Are we, as a company, still living up to it (if we ever did)?
Is the mission growing and pulling us forward?
And lastly, Are we all on this mission together?

CHAPTER 8
