What is the problem that trumps all other problems?
Carl Sagan is remembered by many as the celebrated, slightly geeky
astronomer who spoke of “millions and billions” of stars. But Sagan had
another, more down-to-earth passion, a subject he studied almost as deeply as
the cosmos: critical thinking.
Sagan understood that human beings have a hard time thinking right. In his
writings, he listed and described the many mental traps and logical fallacies
that can cause people to make faulty assumptions, poor judgments, and bad
decisions. Sagan even came up with a set of sensible rules and guidelines—he
called it his “baloney detection kit”—designed to help people think more
clearly.
To Sagan, the stakes were high. As he told an interviewer in 1996, “If we are
not able to ask skeptical questions . . .  to interrogate those who tell us
something is true, to be skeptical of those in authority . . .   then we are up for
grabs for the next charlatan, political or religious, who comes ambling along.”

<!-- PAGE 163 -->
## Source Page 163
Today, decades later, Sagan’s words seem prescient. We have seen large
swaths of people fall prey to conspiracy theories and “big lies” espoused by
politicians, media gures, and others playing the role of Sagan’s “charlatan.”
And even in the larger population, among those not prone to extremist views
or conspiracy thinking, there is widespread confusion about what’s true and
who should be believed. One study found that 80 percent of students—from
middle school all the way through college—could not judge the credibility of a
news story on social media.
How do you turn an assumption into a question?
To be a critical thinker, it’s often necessary to challenge one’s own assumptions—which may require venturing outside your bubble to try to see
things from a diﬀerent perspective. Monica Guzman, a journalist in Seattle, set out to do this a few years ago, partly because she was tired of
hearing fellow liberals in her social circle constantly castigate Trump voters without actually knowing any (this particularly bothered Guzman
because her beloved parents are Mexican immigrants who voted for Trump). So Guzman arranged a ﬁeld trip to a nearby county where most of
the residents were staunch Trump supporters. There, the urban liberals had a sit-down with the rural conservatives and the two groups asked
each other questions about interests, concerns, and values—ﬁnding more points of agreement than disagreement. Guzman’s group learned
surprising things about the motivations of the other group (for example, valid concerns about water regulation had a big impact on how farmers
voted). “We came away with a better understanding of each other,” Guzman says, adding that in these divided times, people must endeavor to
“turn their assumptions into questions.”
e psychologist Daniel J. Levitin, who studies and writes about critical
thinking, believes we have reached a crisis point—exacerbated by a ood of
information (much of it wrong or biased) that is overwhelming people who
aren’t willing or equipped to properly evaluate it. “We’ve become less critical in
the face of information overload,” Levitin says. “We throw up our hands and
say, ‘It’s too much to think about.’ ”
But the cost of doing that is potentially huge. Echoing Sagan, if citizens
aren’t willing to think critically as they choose potential leaders, they’re apt to
choose bad ones. And if they can’t judge whether the information they’re
receiving is sound, they may follow bad advice while ignoring
recommendations that are science-based and solid (and perhaps life-saving).
Moreover, as a society, if we can’t think critically about the problems we
face, it becomes more diﬃcult to reach a consensus on them (much less solve
them). For that reason alone, critical thinking—that boring thing you heard
about and ignored in high school—just might be the critical issue of our times.

<!-- PAGE 164 -->
## Source Page 164
What does all of this have to do with “beautiful questions”? First oﬀ, critical
thinking is all about asking questions—the right questions, at the right time,
and with the right mindset (that last part may be the hardest, as we’ll see). And
if a beautiful question is, as dened earlier, one that can shift thinking and
potentially bring about change, then the questions that drive critical thinking
—questions like Why do I believe what I believe?, What is the evidence behind
this claim?, and What is the opposing view?—certainly qualify. If you use them
well and regularly, they can shift your own thinking. ey can improve your
understanding of the world around you and of the information rushing at you.
And they can change the way you make decisions, large and small.
What is critical thinking? (And why is it so hard to do?)
With critical thinking, nothing is as simple and clear-cut as one might hope, so
it’s not surprising that everyone seems to have a diﬀerent denition of it. Some
place an emphasis on the word “critical,” but critical thinking is not just about
fault-nding or disagreeing. e “critical” in this usage refers to judicious
consideration. And when critical is linked to thinking, it’s particularly about
striving for objectivity—in the form of objective analysis and evaluation.
Along with objectivity, another hallmark of critical thinking is fair-
mindedness—which, to my mind, means going above and beyond cold
objectivity. Being fair in your thinking suggests you have good intentions.
A third element of critical thinking is awareness—of context, of multiple
viewpoints, and of your own thought processes and assumptions. e late
Richard Paul, who headed the Foundation for Critical inking, once said,
“Critical thinking is thinking about your thinking while you’re thinking in
order to make your thinking better.”
So, putting the pieces together, I oﬀer up this denition in the form of a
beautiful question: How might we endeavor to think objectively and in a fair-
minded way, carefully considering evidence and multiple viewpoints, while always
trying to be conscious of our own biases?
at may be a question, but it is also a tall order.
It isn’t easy to think about your own thinking, let alone to question it. It
requires us to see from the outside in—and to look for things, such as our

<!-- PAGE 165 -->
## Source Page 165
biases, that are well hidden. en we must be willing to ask questions about
what we nd. Why am I thinking this way? Can I change it?
is takes both time and eﬀort. For some, thinking hard about thinking—
or thinking hard about anything, really—is a feeling to be avoided.
Psychologists use the term “need for cognition” (NFC) to describe a person’s
inclination to engage in hard or eﬀortful thinking. For “high NFC” people,
critical thinking may be a stimulating mental challenge, but for many others,
it’s more of a pain in the brain.
is doesn’t mean those “low NFC” people aren’t capable of thinking hard
or well; if motivated enough (say, if there’s a tangible reward for solving a
complex problem), they may rise to the challenge. But if not highly motivated,
they’re apt to take mental shortcuts, such as relying on gut reactions and
supercial cues (e.g., “I don’t want to spend time guring out which car makes
the most sense for me—I’m going to just ‘go with my gut’ and buy the one
that gives me a good feeling inside.”).
However, a growing body of research has concluded that our instincts—our
natural hardwired tendencies to think or react in certain ways when faced with
new information—aren’t as trustworthy as we might believe. As the critical
thinking expert Levitin puts it: If you make decisions based on instinct, “your
gut is going to be wrong more than it is right.”
What would Jay-Z do?
Andrew Rossi, formerly of the marketing ﬁrm M Booth and now working at Google, has found that one of the best ways to stoke creativity during
brainstorming sessions is to ask people in the group to think about the problem they’re trying to solve from an unusual perspective. So, for
example, if a company is introducing a new toothpaste, they might ask: How would IKEA tackle a challenge like this? Another approach is to add
an odd constraint, such as What if your idea had to involve speed dating? Or puppets? Rossi’s group sometimes suggests adopting the perspective
of a well-known artist or entertainer: What would Jay-Z do in this situation? How would Dolly Parton think about this? This kind of POV-shifting
inquiry can be used with historical ﬁgures (What would Abe Lincoln decide about this?) or even ﬁctional characters (What would Atticus Finch
think?). And in the business world, a go-to ﬁgure is Steve Jobs: Just asking What might Steve do? can help you tap into your inner innovator.
When we rely on instinct or gut feeling, we can fall into all sorts of traps.
Perhaps the best known of these is conrmation bias, which is the tendency to
focus on information that conrms preexisting assumptions and beliefs. But
we’re also susceptible to many other biases and fallacies. For instance, the
availability bias causes us to rely too much on information that is easily

<!-- PAGE 166 -->
## Source Page 166
available (instead of seeking out better information). A negativity bias may
cause us to give undue weight to negative information (e.g., we might focus on
one failure or crash amid many successes).
David Robert Grimes, who discusses these and other mental traps in his
book Good inking, notes that, “Simply being aware of cognitive pitfalls
makes us less likely to blunder into them.” But to even acknowledge that you’re
susceptible to biases and wrong thinking requires a shift in attitude and
mindset.
Am I thinking like a soldier or a scout?
In order to be able to question your own thinking, you must be open to the
possibility that you might be wrong. For many, this a stopper. It goes against
the common tendency to automatically defend what you believe, without
question. at tendency to be defensive gets in the way of critical thinking.
To illustrate this point, Julia Galef, cofounder of the Center for Applied
Rationality, oﬀers up a clarifying metaphor in the form of a beautiful question.
Galef suggests we ask ourselves Am I a soldier or a scout? She explains that there
is a very diﬀerent mindset for a soldier as opposed to a scout. A soldier’s job is
to protect and defend against the enemy, whereas the scout’s job is to seek out
and understand. ese two distinct attitudes can also be applied to the ways in
which all of us process information and ideas in our daily lives.
e mindset of a scout (or any type of explorer) is rooted in curiosity.
“Scouts are more likely to say they feel pleasure when they learn new
information or solve a puzzle,” according to Galef. “ey’re more likely to feel
intrigued when they encounter something that contradicts their expectations.
And scouts are grounded: eir self-worth as a person isn’t tied to how right or
wrong they are about any particular topic.”
In other words, scouts have “intellectual humility.” Dened as “a state of
openness to new ideas, a willingness to be receptive to new sources of
evidence,” intellectual humility is seen by one of its champions, author and
University of Virginia professor emeritus Edward Hess, as the key to thriving
in days ahead. We can’t compete with articial intelligence unless we humans
keep learning, experimenting, creating, and adapting, Hess says. And we can’t

<!-- PAGE 167 -->
## Source Page 167
do any of that unless we assume the lifelong role of humble inquirer. As Hess
declares: “Humility is the new smart.”
If the “old smart” was about getting high grades, knowing more right
answers, and not making mistakes, the “new smart” is measured by one’s ability
to keep adapting. But to do that, Hess says, we must avoid being overly
invested in our own ideas and expertise. “I must decouple my beliefs from my
ego,” Hess explains. “I must be open-minded and treat my beliefs as
hypotheses to be constantly tested and subject to modication by better data.”
ough humility is often associated with meekness, Hess says we should
think of it as “being open to the world.” “I’ve got to overcome my reexive
ways of thinking—my ego, my fears, my ght or ight responses.” And in that
regard, it can be seen as courageous. If we embrace intellectual humility, he
believes, it can help with everything from innovation to civil discourse because
“it’s no longer about who’s right—it’s about what is accurate.”
Overcoming the urge to “be right” takes a conscious eﬀort, it seems. “We
may need to learn how to feel proud instead of ashamed when we notice we
might have been wrong about something,” Julia Galef says, “or learn how to
feel intrigued instead of defensive when we encounter some information that
contradicts our beliefs.”
Questioning can help; Galef advises people to ask themselves, What do I
most yearn for—to defend my own beliefs or to see the world as clearly as I can?
Another way to phrase this question: Would I rather be right or would I rather
understand? Would I rather be right or would I rather learn? Because it’s harder to
learn and gain a better understanding of an issue or situation if you’re locked
into defending your original position.
Being stuck in the “defensive soldier” position can impact you in various
ways. In studying entrepreneurs, I found the most successful ones were willing
to modify their ideas based on feedback; by being willing to admit their
original idea was not quite right, they became more receptive to possible
improvements. e “need to be right” can also aﬀect personal relationships; it
can keep arguments and feuds going far too long. Oprah Winfrey has written
that “Proving I was right used to be a major character aw,” which she says
impacted friendships by prolonging ghts and misunderstandings. She

<!-- PAGE 168 -->
## Source Page 168
eventually asked herself, Do you want to be right or do you want peace?, and she
opted for the latter.
Why do I believe what I believe? (And what if I’m wrong?)
Having let go of the need to reexively defend your beliefs, you can begin to
objectively question them. ink of it as a three-part journey of inquiry into
your own assumptions: rst, to try to identify them; then to understand them;
and nally, to possibly modify them.
Years ago, the Nobel Prize–winning physicist Arno Penzias was asked what
led to his success. Penzias explained that he made a daily habit of asking what
he called the “jugular question”: Why do I believe what I believe? Penzias felt the
question served as a daily reminder to “constantly examine your own
assumptions,” which in turn helped open him up to new ideas and even to
breakthroughs in his eld (such as his discovery of cosmic microwave
background radiation).
Let’s break Penzias’s jugular question into three parts: the What, the Why,
and the What If. First, the What involves simply trying to identify some of
your biases or assumptions on a topic. So the initial question to ask yourself
would be: What am I inclined to believe about this particular issue? (If you need
help, ask others close to you about which side you tend to land on in various
areas.)
Moving from What to Why, we return to Penzias’s original question—which
tries to get at the basis for whatever feelings or beliefs you might have on this
subject. By thinking about this (and perhaps researching or talking to others
about it), we can begin to see if the belief or gut feeling holds up to scrutiny.
You may realize that the viewpoint was handed down by others. It may stem
from your background, a particular experience you had, the media bubble you
inhabit, or the people with whom you associate. You may also come to nd
that this view has scant evidence to support it. Or that it may be a belief that
made sense once but no longer does. (is is such a common problem that the
author Daniel Pink recommends regularly asking, What did I once believe that
is no longer true?) In questioning why you believe what you believe, don’t

<!-- PAGE 169 -->
## Source Page 169
overlook the “desirability bias,” which may lead you to think something is true
simply because you want it to be true.
After considering the What and the Why of your own beliefs and
assumptions, try asking What If—as in, What if my beliefs or assumptions on
this issue are just plain wrong? In exploring this possibility, Richard Larrick, a
Duke University professor and a leading researcher on the subject of
“debiasing,” says this question works because “it directs attention to contrary
evidence that would otherwise not be considered.”
How might I own my own biases?
To the extent we can gain a glimmer of awareness of our biases—through self-
reection, self-questioning, or other ways—we should make the most of this
self-knowledge. Ask yourself this question: How might I own my own biases?
If we have a sense that we tend to react to certain subjects in predictable
ways, or that we lean a particular way on some issues, we can own that bias by
acknowledging it and trying to factor it in as we take in new information or
make new judgments. We can ask ourselves: Knowing that I tend to lean in one
direction, how might that be altering my view of this new information or situation?
Going a step further, you might ask: Knowing that I lean to one side, how can
I begin to straighten up? Interestingly, you can do that by forcing yourself—if
only temporarily—to lean hard the other way. at’s done by briey
embracing the opposite point of view. Larrick says the “consider the opposite”
approach is standard practice in his eld. I normally think about this issue this
way, but what would “opposite me” think about this issue?
When I rst learned from Larrick about the “consider the opposite”
approach, it immediately reminded me of a classic Seinfeld episode. In it, the
beleaguered character George Costanza is frustrated because his decisions
always seem to turn out wrong. us, he decides that he is going to begin
making all important decisions by rst asking, What would Opposite George do?
—which prompts him to do exactly the opposite of whatever he would
normally do. (In the end, it doesn’t work; George still nds a way to screw up
everything.)

<!-- PAGE 170 -->
## Source Page 170
In real life, the “consider the opposite” strategy is not meant to be followed
blindly; the viewpoint that is opposite yours may not be right, after all. Rather,
it’s designed to open up your thinking to consider other possibilities and beliefs
—and may lead you to a more nuanced and balanced position.
What is the evidence? (And how much does it weigh?)
So far, we’ve looked at questions to ask yourself, as you examine your own
beliefs and biases. But a big part of critical thinking is about analyzing and
making sense of what’s coming at us—the new information, claims, and
viewpoints we encounter.
First, an acknowledgment: Today, it is not easy to vet the information
coming at us, because there is so much of it. And as we ask questions about
incoming claims and news stories, those questions may not be easy to answer
without doing extensive research. But at the very least, we should be aware of
the questions; we should ask and answer them as well as we reasonably can.
Because we’ve learned by now that the old media gatekeepers are gone (for
better or worse) and in the current media landscape, there is no reliable lter to
separate good information from bad—except for the lter within each of us,
the “built-in baloney detector” that runs on skeptical questions.
A critical thinker presented with a claim—whether it’s coming from a
product salesman, a politician, an advertisement, or a news story—habitually
asks, What is the evidence behind this claim and how strong is it? at may lead
to a subset of more specic evidence questions, such as Does this evidence come
from a biased source? Is there an agenda behind it?
Answering these questions may require digging to nd out if, for example,
the source of information has a strong track record for telling the truth or
whether that source may have a special interest in advancing this particular
claim. (In terms of the latter, always ask, Cui bono?—Latin for “Who
benets?”)
Sometimes the problem with information is not what is there, but what’s
missing—whether it’s a news story with insuﬃcient reporting or a sales pitch
that leaves out important details. us, a critical thinker is inclined to ask,
What are they not telling me? when oﬀered potential solutions that may neglect

<!-- PAGE 171 -->
## Source Page 171
to mention side eﬀects, hidden costs, and potential negative consequences. e
good news about this current age of information overload is that it makes it
easier than ever before to check up on claims—to do a search on “potential
side eﬀects of X” or “hidden costs of Y.” But remember that every counterclaim
you nd should also be subject to skeptical questions about its veracity.
When someone is trying to persuade you of something, be on the lookout
for rhetorical “baloney,” as Carl Sagan would put it. Sagan noted that bogus
claims and arguments are often rooted in faulty logic. So the question to be
asked is: Is this logical? Illogical arguments are not supported by sound
reasoning; they tend to be based on fallacies and faulty assumptions. For
example, a common fallacy is the slippery slope argument—wherein someone
tries to convince you that it is risky to do some perfectly reasonable thing
because it will surely lead to something much worse (e.g., one minor
restriction will eventually lead to you losing all your rights!). Other fallacies
include false dichotomies (either you’re with us or against us), and the
bandwagon fallacy (everybody else seems to believe this, so it must be right).
Sagan pulled together an invaluable list of more than a dozen of the most
pernicious logical fallacies to watch out for in his “baloney detection kit,”
which is actually an essay rst published as a chapter of his 1996 book e
Demon-Haunted World: Science as a Candle in the Dark (you can learn more
about Sagan’s list of fallacies at www.amorebeautifulquestion.com/Sagan).
Once you’re aware of these fallacies, you’ll begin to spot them easily.
Previously we talked about the importance of “considering the opposite”
when questioning your own beliefs—and it’s just as important, when
evaluating claims or arguments, to again ask, What’s the other side of this issue?
But when trying to consider the “other side,” keep in mind that it can also be
useful to ask, Is there actually another side? “ere is not another side to the
question of whether we really landed on the moon,” Daniel Levitin points out.
“We did.”
If you decide there is another side of an issue, consider both sides together
and ask, Which of the conicting views has more weight behind it? In the end,
you may be left with a judgment call—as in, “I have three strong reasons to
believe one side and one reason to believe the other; I’ll go with the stronger
case.”

<!-- PAGE 172 -->
## Source Page 172
Indeed, being willing to make those judgment calls is an essential part of
critical thinking. It can be tempting (particularly in our “low NFC” moments)
to say, “ere are reasons to choose A and reasons to choose B—so I can’t
decide!” Or: “Well, both candidates are awed—so it doesn’t matter who I vote
for.” But the critical thinker will make the eﬀort to compare and carefully
weigh the reasons to choose A versus the reasons to choose B—at which point
it usually becomes clear that there is a choice that makes more sense.
How can I be sure I’m not just “JAQing oﬀ”?
e roughly twenty italicized questions presented so far in this chapter can
help you begin to practice critical thinking—“practice” being the operative
word. Critical thinking is a habit that can be formed and strengthened over
time. It isn’t easy; in fact, it goes against some of our natural instincts and
tendencies, so we may have to force ourselves to do more of it. But at some
point, it can start to become almost reexive. Knowing the questions to ask
certainly helps.
But as I mentioned at the start of the chapter, how you ask—more
specically, the attitude and mindset you have, as you ask these types of
questions—is more important than the questions themselves.
Critical thinkers must be open-minded—meaning they must be receptive to
whatever answers come back from their questions, even if those answers
contradict what they previously thought (indeed, a true critical thinker enjoys
being proved wrong, because it means they learned something!). e main
purpose of the critical thinker, the driving force behind the questions, should
be to learn and understand.
Unfortunately, many people use the tools of critical thinking—the skeptical
questions, the demands for more evidence—with an agenda, instead of with an
open mind. is is what the Foundation for Critical inking calls “weak-
sense critical thinking.” And it is a problem, especially in these polarized times.
e behavior is often most prominently on display in the opinion-based
media, where TV and radio hosts routinely use skeptical questions to promote
a partisan point of view—but worse than that, to sow doubt, muddy the
waters, and even to deny reality. Do vaccines really work? Is the pandemic for

<!-- PAGE 173 -->
## Source Page 173
real, or is it all hype? How do we know the election wasn’t rigged? And at the most
extreme level: Did that school shooting really happen? How do we know it wasn’t
staged? Such questions can be contagious; they’re passed from the talking heads
to the online chatrooms to the local school board meeting. Before long, they’re
everywhere.
e late comedian George Carlin, who was featured in chapter 4, used to
urge his audience to “Question everything,” and that mantra always seemed
like pretty good advice to me. But I can’t help wondering what Carlin, a
rigorous critical thinker himself, would make of the current situation in which
people are questioning everything—including reality.
ere is so much of this type of reckless questioning going on that Urban
Dictionary has a term for it: “JAQing oﬀ.” It’s dened as “the act of asking
leading questions to inuence your audience, then hiding behind the defense
that you’re Just Asking Questions.”
at denition points to one of the main reasons why people, especially the
provocateur pundits, will often pose their most outrageous ideas in the form of
a question—because it provides a built-in defense, as in: “I’m just asking
questions! Aren’t people allowed to ask questions anymore?”
To a questionologist, all of this can be disheartening, of course. It shows
how the powers of questioning—to challenge authority and conventional
wisdom, to stir debate, to shift perceptions, to inuence the way people think
—can so easily be misused. And it has caused me to wonder: Is “question
everything” really such a good idea, after all?
After grappling with this question, it seems to me we still should abide by
George Carlin’s rule and be willing to question everything. No subject—not
even sensitive or controversial ones—should be oﬀ-limits. But if we recognize
that the mere phrasing of an idea as an interrogative can have a powerful
inuence on the people around us, then it seems reasonable to suggest that all
of us should try to question responsibly.
How might one dene “responsible questioning”? Here’s my own highly
subjective view: I think as we ask challenging, skeptical questions of the world
around us, particularly in these polarizing times, we should always endeavor to
do so with (1) an open mind, (2) a healthy respect for facts, and (3) authentic
curiosity.

<!-- PAGE 174 -->
## Source Page 174
Open mind has been covered, so let’s move right to facts. Questioning facts
is fair game; scientists do it all the time. And facts do sometimes turn out to be
wrong. But it is crucial to understand that the burden of proof is on the
questioner who is challenging the predominant evidence.
e philosopher Bertrand Russell famously analogized that if someone
claims there is a teapot, too small to be seen, circling the sun, then it is up to
the claimant to furnish the proof—as opposed to expecting others to prove
that a teapot isn’t circling the sun. Denialists and conspiracy theorists often
pose “invisible teapot” questions (What if the election was rigged?) and then act
as if their uninformed, speculative questions demand that someone else
provide answers.
Finally, it’s worth reiterating that the best questions, the most authentic
ones, are rooted in the desire to learn or understand something. If a question
does not have curiosity as a key ingredient, then it may be a “counterfeit
question”—a statement masquerading as a question. e questions posed by
partisan pundits tend to be agenda-driven, not curiosity-driven. But this isn’t
just a problem with pundits; many of us can be guilty, at times, of dressing up
opinionated statements or accusations as questions.
When posing skeptical questions, a simple way to make sure you’re not
“JAQing oﬀ” is to rst ask yourself a series of questions: Is my question really a
question? Am I actually trying to learn or understand something? Has this question
been answered already? And am I open to fairly considering whatever answers I
might get?
How can I be wrong when it feels so right?
In trying to get people to think critically, perhaps the biggest obstacle is this:
ey think they’re already doing it.
If you bounce back and forth between political blogs on the left and on the
right, you’ll notice something peculiar. On both sides of the political divide,
people talk about critical thinking a lot, often to complain that there is a
troubling lack of it in today’s world. But each side thinks the other side is
suﬀering from that lack of critical thinking. “ose other people” are the ones

<!-- PAGE 175 -->
## Source Page 175
falling prey to propaganda, not asking skeptical questions, and failing to make
sound judgments.
“It is very common for someone to believe, ‘ose who disagree with me are
biased, but I am not,’ ” says the critical thinking teacher and author M. Neil
Browne. “It is one of the biggest obstacles to critical thinking.”
Because many people equate skeptical questioning with critical thinking,
they’re apt to believe: “If I am asking skeptical questions about what the
government or the news media is telling me, then that proves I’m a critical
thinker.” But if merely “asking skeptical questions” qualied you as a critical
thinker, it would mean that all the conspiracy theorists and at-earth believers
are critical thinkers (and indeed, they think they are!). What those people can’t
see or choose not to see is that their skepticism only moves in one direction—
and is completely impervious to contrary evidence (which means it’s not so
much skepticism as denialism).
Another part of the problem is that non-critical thinkers get plenty of
reassurance and positive reinforcement from whatever media echo chamber
they’re in. Fellow members of the tribe agree with them, and pass around
sketchy evidence to strengthen the belief that, “We’re right and everyone else is
wrong.” And of course, it doesn’t help that, as the science writer Kathryn
Schulz puts it, “Being wrong feels exactly the same as being right.”
DOES THIS MEAN the cause of trying to promote critical thinking is
hopeless? Not if we can reach people before they’ve hardened their biases over
time and developed bad thinking habits (such as using weak-sense critical
thinking). Which is why it would be rational and sensible to focus much of
our education eﬀorts on the teaching of critical thinking. Except . . .  we don’t
actually do that. Even more worrying, we’re not entirely sure how to do it.
For some, there is even a question of whether we actually want students to
think critically. Some years back, attempts to teach the subject in schools in
Texas were opposed by local politicians—who claimed that teaching students
to think critically could challenge “xed beliefs” and undermine parental
authority.
You might say, as does Carl Hendrick, head of learning and research at
Wellington College, “We’ve failed to apply critical thinking to the question of

<!-- PAGE 176 -->
## Source Page 176
whether such a thing can be taught.”
at may explain why Neil Browne nds that students entering his college-
level critical thinking course tend to have little previous experience with asking
fundamental questions about evidence, logic, and opposing viewpoints. To the
extent the subject is even being taught in lower grades, there’s inconsistency in
how it’s taught—perhaps not surprising, since there are so many diﬀerent
denitions of critical thinking.
According to Daniel Willingham, a professor of cognitive psychology at the
University of Virginia and a leading expert on the teaching of critical thinking,
a major problem is that critical thinking is not a transferable skill—so you can’t
just oﬀer up a class on how to do it and expect that students will then be able
to use critical thinking in other classes, on other subjects. It is a way of
thinking that is “domain specic,” Willingham writes. e way you might use
critical thinking in a science class—the questions you’d ask, the way you’d
evaluate and make sense of the course material—can be very diﬀerent from the
way you might use it in a history class.
How might we close the critical thinking gap?
Colin Seale grew up with a father who was incarcerated for selling drugs. He was often in trouble himself at school, but when the young Colin
tested well enough to be placed in his school’s “gifted and talented” program, everything changed: He was suddenly expected to think for himself,
to debate classmates, even to question the teacher. He got his ﬁrst taste of critical thinking. Today, Seale—a successful attorney as well as an
activist for educational change—is trying to bring critical thinking to all students, regardless of background. Seale notes that only about 1 in 10
educators teach some form of critical thinking—and usually only to privileged students in gifted programs or magnet schools. That’s why Seale’s
thinkLaw, which helps teachers to seamlessly infuse critical thinking instruction into their lessons, is particularly focused on Title 1
(disadvantaged) schools. “My goal is to close the critical thinking gap,” he says. “Why do we assume some kids can’t do this? It’s not a gap based
on their abilities, but rather on our low expectations.”
is doesn’t mean critical thinking can’t or shouldn’t be taught in schools,
only that it must be done right—by thoughtfully adapting and incorporating
the practice into a variety of classes so that students learn how to think
critically in diﬀerent contexts. And it must be continually refreshed over time
so that students keep thinking this way and become more practiced at it. If
critical thinking is a “habit of mind,” as the educator Deborah Meier described
it in chapters 3 and 4, then the habit must be fostered, strengthened, and

<!-- PAGE 177 -->
## Source Page 177
consistently reinforced—from one class to the next, and from one year to the
next.
It’s a lot to ask of educators, who already have too much material to cover.
But the ability of our society to function in years ahead may depend on
whether those teachers can squeeze in lessons and exercises designed to
produce future adult citizens who can actually think for themselves, and do so
with clarity, sound judgment, and fairmindedness.
As for current adult citizens, many of whom never learned what critical
thinking actually is and how it works, a beautiful question concerning them
might be: How might we reach those who’ve wandered oﬀ course and slipped into
various cognitive pitfalls—yet who are absolutely, positively convinced they’re right?
It won’t be easy, and it cannot be done in the ways you might expect—i.e.,
by trying to enlighten them with logic, facts, evidence, and other components
of critical thinking. at doesn’t work. But as we’ll see in the next chapter,
there is one critical thinking tool that crosses over and can be used to inuence
and persuade others, including those who strongly disagree with you. Can you
guess what that tool might be?
OceanofPDF.com

CHAPTER 9
Asking Questions to Engage and

How can questions help to bridge the divide between us?
How might we pry oﬀ the lid and stir the paint?
e late Fran Peavey, a social activist, excelled at what she called “strategic
questioning,” which I would characterize as questioning with an open mind
and a caring heart. Peavey’s questioning left a mark in various far-ung corners
of the world: in the slums of Bangkok, in war-torn Bosnia, in the water of
India’s Ganges River, and in her adopted hometown of Oakland, California.
Peavey was, to put it mildly, a character. A large, exuberant woman (a
journalist who interviewed her wrote that when she laughed, “her esh
wobbles, her chest heaves, her ears bob”), she was a sometime absurdist
comedian as well as a full-time activist. When she traveled, she took with her a
handheld sign, which she’d hold up as she sat in train stations and other
crowded gathering spots, reading AMERICAN WILLING TO LISTEN.
It was an odd come-on, but it worked. People would approach Peavey,
sometimes warily, to nd out what she was up to. Over a couple of decades,
she conducted thousands of interviews this way. “I rened my interviewing

<!-- PAGE 180 -->
## Source Page 180
technique,” she told the Melbourne Age, “asking open-ended questions that
would serve as springboards for opinions and stories—questions such as How
would you like things to be diﬀerent in your life?”
Peavey believed that by employing the right kinds of questions—open-
ended, curious, slightly provocative at times, but never judgmental—one could
have a meaningful dialogue with people who are very diﬀerent from you,
culturally, politically, temperamentally. Such questions could slip under and
around the barriers between people; they could help identify common ground
and shared concerns. And eventually, if the questioning and the discussion
went deep enough, they might begin to resolve conicts and problems.
Why do you hate me when you know nothing about me?
As a young boy who was the only Black member of his Cub Scout group, Daryl Davis was marching with his troop in a parade when he was
attacked by racists throwing rocks and bottles. From that point on, Davis had a question in his head about people who would do such a thing:
Why do you hate me when you know nothing about me? The question puzzled Davis for many years, until, one day, he decided to begin asking it
—directly of people who belonged to the Ku Klux Klan. It started when Davis, an R&B musician, met and befriended a music aﬁcionado who
happened to be a Klansman; the man then connected Davis to other members of the group. Since that time, Davis has befriended dozens of
Klansmen—and has been responsible for many of them leaving the Klan. “I never set out to convert anyone,” Davis says, “but I am the impetus
for their own conversion.” After extended conversation, “they come to the conclusion that there’s a diﬀerent way to think and believe.”
Peavey used her “strategic questioning” to work with people on all manner
of problems. As one news report noted, she helped ai sex workers who were
facing eviction from their neighborhood, worked on a program to feed the
homeless in Osaka, got a botanic garden replanted in the Croatian city of
Dubrovnik, and even helped California skateboarders who were being chased
oﬀ their favorite skating places. One of her more interesting projects was a
public awareness campaign about cleaning up the Ganges River. She used a
series of questions to gain a better understanding of the issue, asking local
residents questions such as: How do you feel about the condition of the river?
How do you explain the condition of the river to your children?
Peavey said she chose her language carefully, trying not to use the word
pollution (which might oﬀend people who believed the river to be holy) and
instead framing the questions and the discussion around “taking care of the
river.” She could tell that people were daunted by the enormity of the task—so

<!-- PAGE 181 -->
## Source Page 181
she began to focus the questions on a more long-term, ongoing objective: How
are you preparing your children to clean up the river?
When Peavey asked that question, people were forced to admit they weren’t
doing anything in this regard. “eir love of the river, their love for their
children, and the void in their answers to that question could not long exist in
the same minds,” Peavey wrote. “e dissonance was too great.”
Parents responded by organizing a poster-painting contest for the children,
around the theme of the health of the river. e plan was to hang the paintings
in public venues so that “adults will see what the children see and be
embarrassed.” In the years that followed, the contest became a large annual
event. But as Peavey writes, the idea didn’t come from her; it came from the
residents themselves—though it seems to have been sparked by Peavey’s
question.
To Peavey, a question could serve as the lever to pry open the stuck lid on a
can of paint. “If we have a longer lever, or a more dynamic question,” she
wrote, it can also be used to “really stir things up.” In this metaphor, what’s
being stirred are the ideas and potential answers that people already have in
their heads; they just need a little mixing to help those thoughts come
together. But Peavey’s approach to questioning also aimed to break down the
“separation” between people based on diﬀering cultures or views—which seems
highly relevant in these polarized times.
When people are looking at issues from very diﬀerent perspectives, it
becomes problematic if one side tries to impose an answer on the other.
Conversation either becomes argument or shuts down altogether. Perhaps the
only way to break the stalemate on even the most divisive issues is to put the
declarative statements on hold and try working on the following: If we don’t
agree on an answer yet, can we at least come to terms on a question?
e previous eight chapters of this book have focused primarily on
questions we ask ourselves—as we try to learn and adapt to change, solve
problems, innovate, become a better leader, and think critically. But
questioning is also a powerful communication tool. On the most obvious level,
it’s a way to extract information from other people. But I want to focus in this
chapter on the surprising ways that questions can deepen your engagement

<!-- PAGE 182 -->
## Source Page 182
with others and, sometimes, enable you to inuence them (hopefully, for the
better).
When formulated and asked the right way, questions can do a few key
things that help to forge a bond, says the scientist Arthur Aron, who has
conducted extensive research in this area. “First, just by asking, you’re showing
that you care about the other person,” Aron says. “Second, the question
encourages that person to reveal something about themselves. And then that
creates an opportunity for you to respond to what they are revealing.”
It’s a three-part formula: Questions show interest, create understanding, and
build rapport. It’s no accident that people with jobs that require them to
quickly establish trusting relationships—therapists, coaches, hostage
negotiators—rely on questions as a primary communication tool. ese
professionals are trained to ask certain types of questions (usually more open-
ended ones that invite a fuller response) in particular ways.
As former head of the counterintelligence behavioral analysis program at the
FBI, Robin Dreeke’s job was to quickly establish rapport and gain the trust of
operatives and potential sources of information. His work revolved around
knowing precisely how to ask a question so that it encouraged someone to
open up, cooperate, and reveal sensitive information. e wording of his
questions mattered greatly, he says, but just as important was his attitude while
asking the questions. Am I genuinely interested in the other person? Am I able to
put my ego aside and suspend all judgment? Am I prepared to truly listen, as
opposed to just acting as if I am listening? “If you don’t do all of these things,”
Dreeke told me, “it can undermine the rapport you’re trying to build with your
questions.”
e good news is that none of the techniques used by Dreeke and other
“professional questioners” are all that technical. Anyone can use them well if
they remain mindful of what they’re asking and how they’re asking it as they’re
asking.
In social interactions, we often misuse questioning: We ask rote, incurious
questions out of habit (How are you?); we ask questions that are really just
critical statements in disguise (What were you thinking?); and we express
opinions or dispense advice in the form of questions (Why don’t you just do
this?). ese incurious questions don’t contribute much to the building of

<!-- PAGE 183 -->
## Source Page 183
relationships. ey don’t show true interest, don’t create understanding, and
don’t build rapport.
To get better at asking the kinds of questions that do forge deeper
connections with the people around us, we need to do a few things. We must
endeavor to ask “authentic” questions rooted in curiosity; try to suspend
judgment and withhold advice as we focus more on inquiry; take a small risk
by being willing to ask open-ended, “deeper” questions (even of people we may
not know very well); and be willing to listen carefully and follow up on what
we’re hearing with questions that gently probe a little deeper. Examples of each
of these approaches follow.
What if I replace judgment with curiosity?
Some years ago, the acclaimed playwright Lynn Nottage became interested in
writing about what happens in a factory town as the jobs begin to disappear.
She searched for a locale that might best encapsulate the changes and struggles
of post-Industrial America, and settled on the steel town of Reading,
Pennsylvania. But before writing about it, Nottage knew she needed to spend
time there talking to the people of Reading face-to-face.
When she rst went to Reading in 2011, she said, “I did not know anything
about the city or anyone in the city—I was confronting it as very much an
outsider.” On the surface, she had little in common with the people she
encountered there. Nottage is a female Black artist who leans to the left
politically. Her interview subjects were mostly white working-class men. (One
of them, she noticed, had white supremacist tattoos.) But as Nottage told the
New York Times, “I like to replace judgment with curiosity. You tell me your
story. I’m going to listen without interruption, and then decide what I think.”
e people in Reading wanted to be heard. “It’s surprising because I thought
that there would be some resistance,” said Nottage, “but I think so few people
actually asked them questions, and asked them questions like, ‘How are you
feeling and what are you experiencing?’ I found that people really sort of
leaned in and responded with a level of honesty that surprised me.” Nottage
also found that when talking with the steel workers, “what they were saying felt
very familiar and struck home with me.” As she told the New Yorker, these laid-

<!-- PAGE 184 -->
## Source Page 184
oﬀ workers felt helpless, ignored, and invisible. “I was sitting with these white
men, and I thought, you sound like people of color in America.”
“I feel that my role as an artist isn’t to come up with solutions, but to ask the
right questions in the right moment,” Nottage says. Just by being willing to sit
with someone of a diﬀerent worldview and listen—while asking the occasional
empathetic question—Nottage was able to gain insight and understanding,
which she could then pass along to her audiences. (And by the way, the
Broadway play that came out of this research, titled Sweat, earned Nottage a
Pulitzer Prize.)
Nottage’s research motto—“replace judgment with curiosity”—is a good
credo for all of us in these divided times. And it also highlights one of the most
essential ingredients of good questioning. If you ask questions with genuine
curiosity, your questions will sound and feel more authentic, and the person on
the receiving end will be more apt to be engaged and even attered.
WE TEND TO think of curiosity as something out of our control—that you
either feel curious about someone/something, or you just don’t. But Jeﬀrey
Wetzler—a fellow questionologist whose forthcoming book Ask is all about the
art of having curious and productive conversations—says we should think of
curiosity as a choice. Before any interaction with another person, maybe we can
“turn up the curiosity dial,” as Wetzler puts it.
is demands that we focus in on the goal of learning and understanding
more about someone. It can have a powerful eﬀect on the other person; “our
desire to understand stirs their desire to tell us more,” says Wetzler. Having the
curiosity dial turned up also can drown out distractions that get in the way of a
good conversation, such as our own anxiety, the desire to impress, or the
tendency to judge.
If your curiosity is dialed up, the person you’re talking with will probably
notice—but just in case they don’t, there are simple ways to clearly signal your
curiosity so your conversation partner doesn’t mistake it for being nosy or
critical. It can be as easy as just putting the words “I’m curious” at the
beginning of a query: as in, I’m curious, why do you take this particular
approach?

<!-- PAGE 185 -->
## Source Page 185
If you really want to make sure there’s no misunderstanding of your motives,
add a rationale onto the end of the question. So now you would be asking: I’m
curious, why do you take this particular approach? e reason I ask is, I’ve been
thinking about changing my own approach. is technique can be thought of as
a “question sandwich”: Lead with curiosity, put the actual question in the
middle, and end with rationale.
As you’re considering possible questions to ask, curiosity should be the main
ingredient; which means, ideally, your questions should be focused on things
you actually want to learn about—stories, interests, dreams, passions.
Fortunately, this also happens to be what people like to share. So when trying
to engage with people you don’t know, opt for questions like What are you
excited about these days? is is a better alternative to the standard What do you
do for a living? question, because not only is What do you do? a rote, supercial
question, but it also forces a person to talk about their job when they may have
more interesting passions to share.
Another question that can draw out someone’s goals or interests is What
problem do you wish you could solve? Leave it open enough that the person can
talk about what they like: from saving the world to shedding a few pounds.
And the conversation easily proceeds from there.
You can almost always improve the questions you ask others by adding
specicity. So instead of asking, How was your day today?, try What made you
smile/laugh today? And take questions that are closed (meaning they call for
simple factual or yes/no answers) and open them up. Instead of asking How
long have you lived in Boise? and Do you like it? (each of which can be answered
in a word or two), try something like What brought you to Boise? and What’s the
most enjoyable thing about living there?
Are you listening for diﬀerences or just similarities?
If curiosity is a primary ingredient of good questioning, listening isn’t far
behind. One of the best ways to demonstrate that you’re truly curious about
and interested in what someone has to say is to actually listen to what they’re
saying.

<!-- PAGE 186 -->
## Source Page 186
Being a good questioner depends on being a good listener because often the
seeds of your next question are contained in what someone is telling you—but
you’ll only notice that if you’re paying attention. And while listening may seem
like an easy thing to do, doing it well—listening actively and deeply—is
actually quite challenging.
Start with the fact that our ability to listen is four times faster than the rate
at which people usually speak—which often causes the listener’s attention to
drift. Research shows that the average person listens with only about 25
percent eﬃciency. But we can improve that by paying close attention not only
to the speaker but to our own listening habits, says Oscar Trimboli, host of the
popular Deep Listening podcast, and author of two books on this subject.
“Most people listen for similarities, not diﬀerences,” Trimboli says. By that
he means that we’re more attuned to statements or ideas that we agree with or
are somehow familiar. We ignore what seems diﬀerent, unfamiliar, unexpected
(which is often where the potential lies for truly interesting and enlightening
conversation). I know exactly what Trimboli is talking about because for many
years as a journalist, I conducted interviews where I was usually listening for
what I wanted to hear: quotes that would conrm or add to the premise of my
story. But when I paid attention to unexpected takes or tangents, that’s when
my articles took the most interesting turns. As Trimboli puts it, “Listening is
the willingness to have your mind changed.”
ough listening is not a competitive sport, we sometimes treat it that way.
e temptation is to ll any opening (even if there is no opening) with our
opinions and stories, as in: How can I top the speaker’s story with one of my own?
But as we’re thinking about that, we’re not paying full attention to the person
speaking. When you nd yourself feeling tempted to interject, you might try
asking yourself the “WAIT question”—the acronym stands for Why Am I
Talking?
It isn’t easy to just shut up and listen because humans are prone to
“conversational narcissism,” a tendency to shift the conversation so that it
focuses on ourselves. Celeste Headlee, radio host and author of the book We
Need to Talk, coined the term and admits she’s prone to it herself. For example,
when her friend’s father had passed away and the friend tried to converse with
Headlee about it, Headlee immediately began to discuss her own experience of

<!-- PAGE 187 -->
## Source Page 187
losing her father. She didn’t understand at rst why her friend was put oﬀ by
this. “I was just trying to be helpful . . .  to say, ‘I know how you feel.’ ” But she
later came to realize that “I was interjecting my story of my own struggle,
when it just needed to be about her.” As Headlee puts it, “A conversational
narcissist is the one that keeps taking the ball from the game of catch and not
ever passing it back.” (Jeﬀrey Wetzler cleverly calls it “Back to Self” or “BS
listening.”)
Rather than thinking of a conversation as a competition, Mark Goulston, a
psychiatrist and author of the book Just Listen, advises us to “think of it as a
detective game, in which your goal is to learn as much about the other person
as you can.” us, instead of asking oneself, How can I score a conversational
point?, a better question is How can I make sure I’m really hearing and
understanding what this person is trying to say?
ere is a questioning technique for making sure you understood what a
person is trying to say—and it’s so basic that you might underestimate its
eﬀectiveness. It’s known as paraphrasing and can be done at various points
during a conversation.
After your conversation partner has expressed a thought, particularly an
important one, you repeat back what has just been said in the form of a
question. (So what you’re saying is, you’re feeling underappreciated and not sure
what to do about it?)
Paraphrasing is eﬀective for two reasons. First, it helps to ensure clearer
communication. You may have misheard what was said, or the person may
have expressed it poorly on the rst try, and this provides an opportunity for
clarication. But it also shows the speaker that you’re actually listening and
making an eﬀort to understand.
Former FBI hostage negotiator Chris Voss says that a shortened form of
paraphrasing known as “mirroring” is a tactic that can help defuse tense
discussions and encourage openness. It involves repeating several key words
that have just been said, phrasing it as a question. For example, if someone
said, “I feel as if nobody in this company cares about all the work I’ve done,”
you’d mirror that by saying, Nobody in the company cares? As Voss explains, the
tactic encourages people to better explain themselves—and makes them feel as
if they’re being heard. An even more concise version of this technique is an

<!-- PAGE 188 -->
## Source Page 188
“echo question,” which focuses on a key phrase or word and repeats it back. In
the example given above, the echo question would be: Nobody? (Note: when
paraphrasing or echoing what someone has said, tone of voice is important; the
idea is to sound interested, not skeptical.)
What is the most underrated type of question?
You could think of the paraphrase as a very simple version of a follow-up
question, which may be one of the most important—and underrated—
questioning tools available to you.
Follow-up questions are important because they can help you better
understand what someone is saying—and also may be used to elicit a fuller,
more meaningful answer. A follow-up question is the shovel that helps you
break through “surface answers” to dig for something deeper.
Why is it underrated? Because a follow-up may be seen as less “original”
than other questions; since it’s based on the previous answer, it’s more of a
reaction than a creation. Interviewers will often spend great amounts of time
preparing a list of questions, but they spend almost no time working on
follow-ups. Indeed, you can’t really prepare specic follow-up questions in
advance. at’s what makes it hard: It requires the skill of actively listening
combined with spontaneous question formulation.
However, your ability to truly engage with people—and going beyond that,
to inuence or persuade them—is extremely dependent on follow-up questions.
Deep Listening podcaster Trimboli recommends using both “north/south”
follow-up questions—which ask for more clarication or detail on what
someone has just said—as well as “east/west” follow-up questions that ask the
speaker to broaden or expand upon what they’ve said with additional ideas and
diﬀerent thoughts.
For example, if someone told you they were feeling underappreciated in
their job, a north/south follow-up question might be, “Can you tell me more
about that?” An east/west follow-up would be, “Anything else bothering you,
besides that?” One is digging down deeper, the other is staying on topic but
shifting direction slightly to try to cover additional ground.

<!-- PAGE 189 -->
## Source Page 189
“Tell me more” is one of the most useful and powerful follow-up questions.
And yes, it is a question—a request for information—even though that
wording can make it seem like a command. As a sidenote: It’s not absolutely
necessary, but I prefer to keep “Can you” at the beginning just so that it sounds
more like a question. Trimboli often uses some version of “tell me more” to
alternatively zero in on his interviewee’s feelings (Can you tell me more about
how that felt?) or thoughts (What were you thinking at the time?), making for a
richer conversation.
Can questions make you fall in love?
The scientist Arthur Aron wondered: Was there a way, in a laboratory setting, to create instant intimacy between strangers? He took pairs of
strangers into his university lab and tried to get them to like, or possibly even love, each other. Gradually, Aron discovered a powerful force that
seemed able to produce the desired eﬀect: not a love potion, but a well-crafted and strategically designed series of questions. Aron would give a
list of the same questions to each member of the participating pairs. The partners would then take turns asking each other the questions and
responding. Aron eventually came up with 36 questions, to be used sequentially. The list began with more superﬁcial queries (e.g., Who would be
your ideal dinner guest?) and then built to much more personal questions probing deep feelings about hopes, regrets, dreams, core values. When
people questioned each other in this way, the results were surprising—even to Aron. Most of the pairs of strangers came out of the session with
highly positive feelings for each other; one couple later married. See the full list of Aron’s 36 “love questions” at
www.amorebeautifulquestion.com/36-questions
Moving from north/south to east/west, one of the most eﬀective follow-up
questions you can ask consists of three simple words: And what else? Michael
Bungay Stanier, a renowned executive coach, calls it the “AWE question” and
considers it to be “the best coaching question in the world.” Just asking And
what else? nudges people to go beyond top-of-mind answers and elicits more
(and usually better) ideas and insights from your conversational partner. It
encourages the process of “thinking out loud” about a challenging subject.
Bungay Stanier oﬀers two caveats about the AWE question, however. One:
e follow-up must be asked with genuine interest. If it is asked as nothing
more than a rote question, then it becomes irritating. And, two, it generally is
most eﬀective when asked two or three times in succession, but no more than
that. By the third round, consider rewording the question slightly to: Is there
anything else?—which invites closure.
e AWE question can be useful when talking to a friend or family member
who’s having a problem, but it’s also a powerful tool for business managers. In

<!-- PAGE 190 -->
## Source Page 190
trying to diagnose a problem at work, it’s not uncommon for managers to ask:
What’s the issue here? or What’s causing the problem? But because people
sometimes have trouble articulating a diﬃcult or sensitive issue on the rst try,
it may take a couple of follow-up “AWE questions” before the real issue
surfaces. Similarly, the question can be a tool for encouraging more creative
thinking about solutions, as in: What could we try in order to solve this problem .
. .  and what else? or What issues should our company be thinking about . . . and
what else?
What if I advise less and inquire more?
If your questioning can get someone to begin to open up about something
important to them—challenges they may be facing, changes they’d like to
make in their lives—it might seem like a good time to oﬀer your help in the
form of advice. We all like to give advice; it feels good. Bungay Stanier says,
“When you’re giving advice, you’re in control of the conversation. You’re the
one with the answers.”
But people who are experts at using questions to build rapport will tell you:
Resist the urge to begin doling out advice. e aim may be to be helpful, but
the truth is, people’s advice often isn’t as good as they think it is. e advice
giver may not know enough about what’s going on in a situation—the history,
the context—and may be trying to solve a problem that isn’t the real problem.
And the advice giver has their own biases, experiences, and beliefs about how
to deal with a given situation—which means the advice might make sense for
them, but not necessarily for others.
What’s the alternative? Rather than handing people what you think is “the
answer,” it’s preferable to help them nd their own answer—and one way to do
that is through a combination of listening and asking questions that gently
probe and guide. e model for this type of interaction is used by many life
coaches, consultants, and especially therapists. Good therapists don’t tell you
what to do; they lead you on a path to guring it out for yourself.
If you can help someone to think about a problem more clearly and gently
guide them in the direction of possible solutions, you’re leaving room for that

<!-- PAGE 191 -->
## Source Page 191
person to arrive at their own insights and make their own decisions—so that
they have more “ownership” of potential solutions.
What follows is an example of how to do this, shared by Hal Mayer, who
served as executive pastor and leadership trainer at the Church at the Springs
in Ocala, Florida. Mayer was coaching a woman who needed to attract more
volunteers to help in her parish. He started by asking her to set a goal (attract
ten new volunteers). He next asked, What have you tried?, and she mentioned
past eﬀorts to recruit volunteers that had not worked. He then asked this
question: If you could try anything and money was not an object, what would you
do to nd new volunteers?
e woman came up with the idea of oﬀering people $100 to volunteer.
Mayer made note of that and asked, And what else? With each subsequent idea
she shared, he followed up by asking for another idea (And what else?), and
then another. He then showed her the list of several ideas she’d come up with
and asked: Which one of these most interests you—which one would you like to
discuss further? She chose an idea about setting up a lemonade stand at which
kids could hand out applications to volunteer.
Mayer then asked several practical questions about that idea: How would you
set it up? What would you need to get started? What problems might get in the way
of this idea? What are the rst steps you can take, right away? By the time he was
nished with the conversation—which took less than twenty minutes—the
woman had a plan of action and was ready to begin in a few days.
As Mayer pointed out, he did not pass judgment on any of her ideas or try
to tell her how to proceed. “All I did,” he said, “was ask her questions to help
her draw focus.”
One of the important things Mayer did in the midway point of that
conversation was to solicit multiple ideas (using the AWE question). e
favorite idea, about the lemonade stand, wasn’t the rst or even the second idea
mentioned by the woman. It had to be drawn out with follow-up questioning.
THE APPROACH MAYER took here is similar to an established technique
used by psychologists, called Motivational Interviewing. It was initially used to
help people struggling with alcohol abuse; today, psychologists use variations
of it to help patients deal with all kinds of issues. e technique is based

<!-- PAGE 192 -->
## Source Page 192
around asking open-ended questions to identify why and how someone might
wish to make a diﬃcult change in their lives. en, the questioner uses
“reective listening” (similar to paraphrasing) to move the conversation
forward, clarify the person’s thoughts, and provide aﬃrmation. It ends with
summarizing the conversation and asking about possible next steps the person
could take to begin to make actual changes.
You don’t have to be a psychologist to use motivational interviewing.
William R. Miller, one of the pioneers of the practice and coauthor, with
Stephen Rollnick, of the book Motivational Interviewing, oﬀers a simplied
version you can try with a friend, family member, or coworker. If someone you
know is considering making a change in their life, try asking the questions
below and “just listen carefully to their answers,” Miller says.
1. Why might you want to do (this change)?
2. If you did decide to do this, how could you go about it in order to
succeed?
3. What would you say are the three best reasons for you to do it?
4. On a scale from 0 to 10, where zero means “not at all important” and
ten means “extremely important,” how important is it for you to make
this change? What number would you say? And why that number?
“Listen well without interrupting or giving any opinion or advice,” Miller
says. “en give the person a summary of what they said. Finally, ask, So what
do you think you’ ll do?, and again, just listen.”
Motivational interviewing has proven very successful over the years—and
the key to its success may come down to one basic concept, an idea expressed
several centuries ago by the philosopher Blaise Pascal: “People are generally
better persuaded by the reasons which they have themselves discovered than by
those which have come into the mind of others.”
How can questions help to bridge the divide between us?

<!-- PAGE 193 -->
## Source Page 193
Blaise Pascal’s idea that it’s hard to persuade anyone of anything—unless you
can somehow get them to persuade themselves—is particularly relevant in
current times. At dinner tables and in Internet chat rooms and comment
sections, many of us endeavor to convince people with opposing views that
they are wrong—and the arguments have become louder and nastier. Each side
is armed with an arsenal of facts, logic, and appeals to reason. And yet, it
seems, no one ever gains or gives any ground.
“When we argue, we throw all the facts into the arena and let them ght
each other,” says David McRaney, host of the podcast You Are Not So Smart
and author of the book How Minds Change. But facts don’t work when it
comes to changing someone’s mind. One reason is that two people who feel
diﬀerently about an issue can look at the same fact and see it diﬀerently. In
fact, as McRaney points out, even the same person can view a fact in diﬀerent
ways depending on how they’re feeling at the time. For example, when people
are in the rst blush of being in love, McRaney notes, they may look at their
partner’s quirks and habits as things they love about the person. But then,
when they fall out of love, those same quirks become annoyances. “Yet they’re
still the same things they were before,” McRaney points out. ey’re just being
seen in an entirely diﬀerent emotional light.
For a while, McRaney believed that because people are so wedded to their
feelings and have such little regard for facts, it was pointless trying to persuade
them to change their minds. But a few years ago, he noticed something
interesting: Public opinion in the U.S. on the subject of gay marriage seemed
to ip, almost overnight; a majority that had been against gay marriage in
2012 was for it in 2013. And it led McRaney to ask, What happened in the
minds of those people who switched their position?
In his search for an answer to that beautiful question, he came upon a group
of LGBTQ activists who were using an interesting approach to try to inuence
the public on this issue. Members of this activist group, called the Leadership
LAB, were going door to door, having conversations with people, many of
whom had previously voted against gay marriage when it was on the ballot in
California. e canvassers didn’t try to argue with these people or persuade
them; they merely asked questions and listened.

<!-- PAGE 194 -->
## Source Page 194
Canvassers would ask people about when they rst heard of this issue, and if
they personally knew anyone aﬀected by the issue. ey invited the people
being interviewed to share stories and experiences. ey asked them to try to
break down and quantify their own feelings on the issue, using a numerical
rating. Mostly they listened, paraphrased, and reected back the words and
feelings of the interviewee.
e approach came to be known as “deep canvassing.” It used some
techniques similar to that of motivational interviewing, but also mixed in
methods that the canvassers had developed in the eld. And it was
astoundingly successful—so much so that the Leadership LAB began drawing
press attention from around the world. McRaney watched videotapes of many
interviews in which a canvasser had subtly inuenced someone to change their
opinion—often in the course a short conversation. Without even seeming to
realize it, McRaney says, the interviewees “talked themselves into a new
position.”
McRaney, who’d spent years studying the ways people think, almost couldn’t
fathom what he was observing. As he describes it in How Minds Change: “I
imagined opinions building up for years like barnacles on a ship, every day
growing denser, resisting eﬀorts to chip at them. en one day, along comes a
stranger with a clipboard who cleanly wipes it all away—just by asking some
questions and listening.”
WE ALL HAVE it within us, says the radio interviewer Krista Tippett, “to
formulate questions that invite honesty, dignity, and revelation.” I think of
such questions as “bridge questions”—because they can help us traverse the
divide that separates us from others. If you’d like to try building a question
bridge that might help you reach out to someone you’ve been having trouble
connecting with (be it a coworker you tend to disagree with or an uncle with
sharply diﬀerent political views), here are a few ideas.
You might begin by asking yourself a question: Why am I trying to cross this
particular divide? It’s often a worthwhile and admirable thing to attempt—but
just make sure you’re doing it for the “right” reasons. ose might include
trying to repair or strengthen a personal relationship that’s important to you;
trying to promote civil discourse and greater understanding among people

<!-- PAGE 195 -->
## Source Page 195
within your circle at work, among friends, or at home; or, it could be that you
want to broaden your own thinking.
What would you do to reach yourself?
As a pastor of a bible church in a drug-riddled Philadelphia neighborhood known as the Badlands, Joel Van Dyke was determined to reach the
youth of that community—but for years he had no luck ﬁguring out how to do so. Then, after stumbling upon the E. E. Cummings line about
“beautiful questions,” Van Dyke decided to use questioning as an outreach tool. Instead of trying to tell local youths that he knew what they
needed, “I decided to ask, What would you do to reach yourself?” Van Dyke’s willingness to immerse himself in the community and ask that
question led to a surprising conversation. Community youths (including gang leaders) told him they very much wanted a place to play handball,
but had been locked out of the local facilities. “Throw a big handball tournament,” they told Van Dyke, “and we’ll bring all our friends.” Van Dyke’s
church went on to sponsor four tournaments a year, which also provided a venue to share the ministry’s message.
However, if you’re planning to cross that divide just so you can convert
someone on the other side to your point of view, forget about it. It probably
won’t work—and you may end up doing more harm than good to the
relationship.
Decide, at the outset, that you’re going to be driven by curiosity—and that
your “guiding question,” throughout this interaction, will be: What can I learn
from this person who sees things diﬀerently than I do?
As you begin asking questions, lead oﬀ by announcing your curiosity and
end with your rationale for asking this question (the “question sandwich”). I’m
genuinely curious about why you believe what you believe—and the reason I’ d like
to understand this is because I want to try to see these issues from a diﬀerent
perspective.
Try to focus on how each of you came to hold such diﬀering positions,
suggests McRaney. “e idea is to have two people go shoulder to shoulder,” he
says, “and try to understand: Why do you think we disagree? What are the
motivations, the assumptions and prior experiences that led each of us to this point?
How are they diﬀerent?”
It’s important to note that as you try to nd possible areas of agreement, this
does not mean you have to back down from your own stance or beliefs. It isn’t
necessary to agree with someone else’s overall position; the idea is to try to nd
some element of their belief that seems reasonable and understandable. It
could be their intentions, their concerns, or their values.

<!-- PAGE 196 -->
## Source Page 196
Values is a particularly rich area to mine: Research shows that if you simply
get people talking a bit about their values—how they feel about, say, family,
honesty, being a good neighbor—they subsequently tend to become less
argumentative and more reasonable.
Questions are a great tool for nding common ground. James Ryan,
president of the University of Virginia, often relies on the question: Couldn’t we
at least agree that ___? (You can ll in that blank with anything that seems like
a reasonable point both sides can accept.) According to Ryan, “Asking Couldn’t
we at least agree? is a way to push back against polarization and extremism,
because it is an invitation to nd some areas of consensus.” He told me he uses
the question “whenever there’s an impasse.” For example, during heated
discussions with fellow educators about diﬀerent teaching methods, Ryan may
ask something like Couldn’t we at least agree that everyone in this room wants to
improve education for our students? at question can lower the temperature in
the room and help move the conversation forward.
Challenge your conversational partner to shift their thinking, just a little—
and challenge yourself to do likewise. Here are two great questions that can
help with that: After you’ve inquired about the other person’s views and shared
your own, try asking, Can you nd anything in your position that gives you pause?
en follow that up with: Is there anything in my position that you are attracted
to or nd interesting? And then share your own answers to those two questions.
is forces both of you to acknowledge aws in your own argument and
strengths in the opposing one. (A version of these two questions is in Krista
Tippett’s book Becoming Wise.)
As you’re evaluating each other’s positions, try using the numerical rating
technique from the motivational interviewing approach. Ask your companion
to rate their feelings or their position on an issue on a scale of one to ten. For
example: On a scale of one to ten, how do you feel about regulation of guns? (One
= no regulation at all; ten = heavy regulation.)
Researchers have found that even when people are rating something they
disagree with or don’t like, they rarely pick the most extreme number. ey’re
more likely to cite a number like two to three on the low end or eight to nine
on the high end. In which case, you can follow up by asking, Why did you pick
two or three, instead of the lowest number? To answer that question, the person is

<!-- PAGE 197 -->
## Source Page 197
almost forced to come up with something positive about the opposing position
—which means they are beginning to articulate the other side of their own
argument.
Lastly, take it slow—don’t expect to reach total agreement on anything
quickly. “Science Guy” Bill Nye reminds us that we must be patient with those
whose views diﬀer from our own. “We tend to say, ‘Look at the facts! Change
your mind!’ But it can take people a couple of years to change their mind.”
In the meantime, through questioning and listening, we can at least begin to
open up a dialogue—and start to gain some glimmer of an understanding of
the people around us. As Nye suggests, we need to begin saying to each other,
“We’re all in this together—let’s learn together.”

CHAPTER 10
Questioning for Life
If you cannot answer, can you at least respond?
Why are you evading inquiry?
How can I warm up my questioning muscle?
What if I apply questioning to my daily life?
