If you cannot answer, can you at least respond?
When Ariel Burger was fteen years old, he was grappling with deep questions.
A student at an Orthodox Jewish school, he studied the Talmud. But in the
margins of that sacred text, he drew sketches of comic book superheroes. “I
was the artsy kid and I would get in trouble for it,” he says. “I had a lot of
questions about the relationship between tradition and creativity.”
Burger felt torn between the disciplined life of religious observance and the
possibility of living more freely as an artist. “And I couldn’t speak about these
questions to anyone,” he says, “until I met Elie Wiesel.”
Wiesel, middle-aged at the time, was world famous: a Holocaust survivor
who, as a teenager, was sent to the Auschwitz concentration camp, where his
mother and sister were murdered. After liberation, Wiesel didn’t speak of the
atrocities for a decade. en he began to share his story—in his writing, and as
a teacher and, eventually, a global activist. He wrote best-selling books and met
with world leaders. He was awarded the Nobel Peace Prize in 1986, a few years
before the day when the teenage Burger went, with his stepfather, to a lecture
Wiesel was giving in New York.

<!-- PAGE 200 -->
## Source Page 200
At a reception line after the talk, the shy Burger tentatively approached
Wiesel—who extended a friendly handshake and engaged him in conversation.
After a few minutes, Wiesel had to move on to other guests, but rst he oﬀered
an invitation to Burger: “Come and see me—and bring a question.”
It took more than six months for Burger to gather, as he puts it, “the holy
chutzpah” to go see Wiesel at his home in New York. “And I didn’t bring one
question,” says Burger. “I brought many.”
To his surprise, Wiesel did not oﬀer wise answers to each question; “he
didn’t answer any of them,” Burger says, adding that he later realized that “this
is a very Jewish response to questions. When you’re asked a question, in Jewish
life, you respond by asking another question—and the questions build on one
another and they lead somewhere. His questions and stories, in response to my
questions, were much more helpful than any answers could have been.”
Burger continued to have occasional question sessions with Wiesel over the
next few years, then enrolled in Wiesel’s courses at Boston University. He
would go on to become a teaching assistant for Wiesel while earning his
doctorate degree—all the while absorbing Wiesel’s wisdom and one
particularly important lesson: Wiesel demonstrated that “you could bring your
questions into a life of faith,” Burger says. “at was very liberating for me.”
Burger did manage to strike that balance between a life of observance and
one of creativity. He became a rabbi while also pursuing his creative passion as
an artist and writer (and remaining in close contact with Wiesel until his death
in 2016). e next year, Rabbi Burger published the book Witness: Lessons from
Elie Wiesel’s Classroom. He continues to share the lessons from that book—
many of which revolve around the importance of questions—with audiences
around the world, particularly educators.
ELIE WIESEL FELT “there was something holy in questions,” Burger says. As
an activist for peace, he believed that questions could bring people together
(“It is the answers that divide them,” Wiesel said). Wiesel also felt that
questions could be an inspiring, motivating force in people’s lives—if they were
willing to ask themselves the challenging ones. “ere is quest in questions,”
Wiesel said. is had been true in his own life—he was driven by several
complex, ambitious questions that were at the center of his own quest. One of

<!-- PAGE 201 -->
## Source Page 201
those questions: How can human beings do such terrible things to other human
beings—and how can we change that?
What is your sentence?
This is a favorite question of the author Daniel Pink, though he acknowledges in his book Drive that it can be traced back to the journalist and
pioneering congresswoman Clare Booth Luce. While visiting John F. Kennedy early in his presidency, Luce expressed concern that Kennedy might
be in danger of trying to do too much, thereby losing focus. She told him “a great man is a sentence”—meaning that a leader with a clear and
strong purpose could be summed up in a single line (e.g., “Abraham Lincoln preserved the Union and freed the slaves”). Pink believes this concept
can be useful to anyone, not just presidents. Your sentence might be “He raised four kids who became happy, healthy adults,” or “She invented a
device that made people’s lives easier.” If your sentence is a goal not yet achieved, then you also must ask: How might I live up to my own sentence?
e rst part of that question, in isolation, might have been disheartening—
but the second part was a bold, optimistic call to action. It was Wiesel’s life
mission to nd ways to respond to that question; not necessarily to answer it,
but to respond to it.
ere is a crucial diﬀerence there, and it is at the heart of Wiesel’s
philosophy about questioning. You don’t have to have the answer to life’s big,
diﬃcult questions; but you must be willing to ask them, think about them,
and respond to them—meaning, to act on them in some positive way. You
may not have an answer to the hunger problem; but it doesn’t mean you can’t
nd ways to feed people.
We are addicted to answers, Wiesel believed, and the addiction can paralyze
us. We may nd ourselves waiting for a single denitive answer that never
materializes; or there may be too many answers, that conict with one another
and create a stalemate. In the meantime, nothing happens; we do not try, we
do not learn, we don’t make any progress on that question or on the problem
that may lurk behind the question.
“Professor Wiesel would say, ‘Stop looking for an answer, look for a
response,’ ” Burger says. Can you embrace that unanswered question and begin to
act on it, somehow?
Why are you evading inquiry?

<!-- PAGE 202 -->
## Source Page 202
is nal chapter is about embracing a life of questioning—about nding the
time and the will to ask more questions of others, and, particularly, of oneself.
I believe all of us can be empowered by the questions we ask ourselves. We can
use self-questioning to get a diﬀerent perspective on a diﬃcult issue; or to
break down a bigger, overwhelming question (What is my true passion, my
higher purpose?) into smaller, more manageable parts; or to give ourselves the
motivation to tackle something we’ve been avoiding, such as getting rid of a
bad habit. In this chapter we’ll look at various types of questions that can be
used in these ways. And we’ll close by considering how you might formulate
and pursue a single “beautiful question”—one that could potentially guide and
inspire you for years to come.
But rst let’s identify a few of the things that tend to stop people from
asking themselves important, meaningful questions. One of them has already
been articulated by Elie Wiesel and Ariel Burger: People are reluctant to ask
themselves challenging questions because they fear they won’t have a good
answer. But Wiesel shows that you can be fueled by big, unanswered questions
and respond to them in powerful ways—as he did in his work as a writer and
humanitarian activist.
e pursuit of unanswered questions can be a driving force for so many
artists, as well as for scientists and entrepreneurs. e stories in the earlier
chapters of the book and in many of the sidebar boxes throughout feature
people who had a question with no immediate answer. ey took ownership of
the question, acted on it—and eventually, something important came of it. So
if you have big questions on your mind that don’t have answers, know that you
are in very good company.
Now we come to a second reason why people avoid asking themselves
meaningful questions. Who has the time? Or to put it another way, in the words
(again) of George Carlin: “Some people see things as they are and ask Why;
Some people see things as they might be and ask Why not; Some people have
to go to work and don’t have time for all that sh*t.”
e joke—which is not indicative of Carlin’s own attitude about
questioning, something he always made time for—is a recognition that, Sure,
we could all sit around pondering deep questions, but where does that t in our
busy lives?

<!-- PAGE 203 -->
## Source Page 203
It ts into the openings we make for it, if we’re willing to make them. And
generally, we make time for the things we value. So the real question is, Do we
value questioning enough to devote any time to it? I’ve devoted the previous nine
chapters to making the case that questioning enables us to learn, solve
problems, innovate, adapt to change, become better leaders, communicate
more eﬀectively with other people, and think critically so that we can make
better decisions. So there’s that. Is it worth a few minutes a day?
If the answer is yes, then it becomes a question of when to do it. Early
morning and late night are prime times for self-questioning, with each having
its advantages. Morning is often when creative juices are owing (and
questions go well with coﬀee). But nighttime is also a good quiet time for
reection and inquiry—and it can sometimes pay oﬀ in the form of a next-day
epiphany. e tech entrepreneur Reid Hoﬀman, a cofounder of LinkedIn,
spends a few minutes each night before bed asking himself questions about a
particular problem or challenge he’s working on. Hoﬀman nds that when he
does this, insights and answers often come to him during the night, making
those few minutes spent on questioning a worthwhile investment.
How much time is needed to have a good, productive think on questions? I
need at least ten minutes just to gure out what question I want to think about
—then another twenty to have any productive thoughts on it. But there’s no
formula. And my fellow questionologist Hal Gregersen, a former MIT
professor and author of Questions Are the Answer, has pointed out that even if
you only devote four minutes a day to thinking of questions to ask yourself, it
can be productive—and it adds up to twenty-four hours (a full day’s worth of
questioning) over the course of a year.
e important thing is to start questioning now, as opposed to postponing it
for a “better” time. e Silicon Valley venture capitalist Randy Komisar talks
about the “deferred-life plan”—wherein ambitious young entrepreneurs devote
themselves entirely to making money in the present, so that at some later point
they’ll have the means to pursue what really matters to them (once they take
the time to gure out what that is). e same attitude can be found anywhere
people are focused on building nancial security so they can be in a
comfortable position when they get around to asking what they actually want to
do with their lives.

<!-- PAGE 204 -->
## Source Page 204
But as Komisar notes, most of the best-laid deferred-life plans don’t go as
planned. e world changes, the big idea zzles, a radical midcourse
adjustment is needed. Like it or not, those questions you’ve been putting oﬀ for
later may suddenly become urgent—so why not get started thinking about them
now?
ere is another reason people evade inquiry (besides not having answers
and not having time): ey simply may not know the right question(s) to ask
themselves. is is an easy problem to solve: ere is no right question. ere
are many interesting questions worth pursuing (and many not worth thinking
about more than two seconds). You will probably know you’ve hit upon a
promising question as soon as you think of it. Great questions have an eﬀect.
ey spark your curiosity; they re the imagination. To quote the renowned
tech writer Kevin Kelly, a great question “is one you badly want answered once
you hear it, but had no inkling you cared [about] before it was asked.”
How can I warm up my questioning muscle?
To get to the good questions you may have to work your way through some
not-so-good ones. And as with so many things, you tend to get better at
questioning as you do more of it. To begin developing your capacity to think
of many questions, quickly, on a given topic, try the Question Formulation
Technique exercise discussed in chapter 3. In that chapter, I talked about use of
the QFT in schools, with small groups of students. But you can use a variation
of it as an adult, with others or by yourself.
Start by thinking of a “Question Focus”—a premise or statement, in two or
three words, that can provide a focal point for generating questions (e.g.,
“Technological change” or “A balanced life”). Don’t use a question as a starting
point—it’s easier to form questions around a statement or a phrase.

Is it time to retire the concept of retirement?
The aging of the Baby Boomers raises the question Can we still aﬀord to have so many people retire in their sixties? Moreover, Is retirement really the
most satisfying, productive way to spend one’s later years? Marc Freedman of CoGenerate.org looks at the growth of the over-sixty population and
asks: Why can’t we turn this dependence into abundance? Freedman thinks older workers have a wealth of experience and knowledge that could
be used in “encore careers” with nonproﬁts, charities, and schools, all of which have a growing need for high-level skills. Freedman’s organization
oﬀers support, job leads, fellowships, handbooks, and classes, readying millions of longer-living boomers to be a vital workforce for change. He
also believes we should encourage people of all ages to plan for the “encore” stage of life by setting up Individual Purpose Accounts (patterned
after IRAs) to help cover the inevitable costs of transitioning and retooling.
Next, set a time limit of, say, ten or fteen minutes, and try to generate as
many questions related to this issue as you can, writing down each question.
e idea is to just keep inquiring about the topic from diﬀerent angles. When
the time expires, count your questions. en try opening up the closed
(yes/no) questions, while closing down the open ones. Lastly, select the three
questions you like best from the list. is exercise seems simple, but it can
really strengthen your ability to “think in questions.”
After you’ve tried formulating questions on a topic, try focusing on a
problem—and this time, use a specic set of questions, designed for problem-
solving. As to what problem to take on, look for any situation that could stand
improvement (e.g., e front door of the building is hard to open; e line
waiting for coﬀee is too long; Our family never seems to have dinner together).
Next, subject the chosen problem to a Why/What If/How cycle of
questioning. Remember, we can use Why questions to help us understand a
problem; What If questions help us imagine possible alternatives; and How
questions lead us toward a practical solution.
Focusing on the problem you’ve identied, start by inquiring about why this
problem exists—and why it hasn’t been solved already. en use your
imagination to brainstorm multiple What If possibilities (What if we tried X?
What if we tried Y?). Next, pick the What If idea you like best and reframe it as
a How question—as in, How might I take this “what if?” idea and begin to
actually make it a reality? How would I take the rst step?
By the end of the exercise, you may or may not have found a practical
solution to the problem (some problems are more resistant than others), but
you will have gotten your rst taste of using questioning the way innovators

<!-- PAGE 206 -->
## Source Page 206
often use it. You can apply this basic framework to all kinds of problems at
home and at work and at the very least it will help you see those problems
more clearly—and perhaps identify possible ways to address them.
As you’re looking around for things to ask questions about, try turning your
Why and What if questions on your own assumptions and beliefs—a great way
to begin honing your critical thinking skills. Start by identifying something
you think you know. en set out to prove yourself wrong, through
questioning. e rst question to ask is the Arno Penzias “jugular” question
from chapter 8: Why do I believe what I believe? Break this belief down with
questions such as: Why (and when) did I rst start believing this? How did this
belief get shaped; what might have been some of the inuences behind it? And
then: What if I get myself to think about this from a completely diﬀerent point of
view? What if I try to make a case for the opposite view or position on this issue?
* * *
THE PREVIOUS EXERCISES are focused on self-questioning, but you can
also begin working on your interpersonal questioning skills. Start with people
you know: your spouse, best friend, family member. Instead of asking the usual
rote questions (How was your day?), aim for something more specic: What’s
the most interesting thing that happened to you today? Or try a more ambitious
question like What are you passionate or excited about these days? Focus on your
listening skills, using paraphrasing, and try to ask follow-up questions that
draw out more details and feelings.
As you build questioning courage, take your inquiry to the outside world.
At the coﬀee shop, pay attention to what the barista does and politely ask, I’m
curious about that thing you just did when you were making the latte—why do you
do it that way? You’ll nd that true curiosity and interest are usually welcomed
by others.
What if I apply questioning to my daily life?
Having done some warm-ups, you can tackle something more substantive:
trying to use questioning to create a change (even just a small one) in your life.

<!-- PAGE 207 -->
## Source Page 207
A great example of how to do this comes from the humorist writer A.J. Jacobs,
who loves to question his everyday habits and experiment with small changes.
As part of an article for Esquire magazine, Jacobs set out to catalog
everything he did during a typical day—asking himself why he made each
decision, no matter how small. Why did he use Crest toothpaste? inking about
it, he realized, “It was because I had some friends at camp when I was twelve
years old who used to tell me it was a cool toothpaste. at’s literally why I’ve
used it for thirty years.” Such was the case with many of his daily activities and
choices: “You discover that we do so many things by rote.”
Jacobs believes every now and then one should go through one’s day, from
waking up until bedtime, questioning and reexamining everything. “One of
my thoughts is that all of this is like when you’re skiing and the skis create ruts
and it becomes easier to just follow those,” he says. But if you keep changing
things up, “it keeps you from staying in those ruts and allows you to see the
world in diﬀerent ways.”
e small changes could be in the route you take to work. It could be
something done around the house, such as the way you make the bed.
Cooking is a great opportunity for experimentation; the chef Chris Young told
me that he tries to remind people, “You have a wonderful lab in your home
that’s ideal for experimenting—it’s called the kitchen.”
e small change could be in the way you dress, the way you x your hair:
Why do it the same old way? What if you tried something diﬀerent? Jacobs told me
about a woman he interviewed who said that on certain occasions she tries to
nd small things she could do in a slightly diﬀerent way—“so instead of
putting her lipstick on clockwise, she would put it on counterclockwise. Just
being more aware of what you’re doing, more mindful—there’s something
wonderful about that.” And when you change one small thing and it works, it
can help breed the condence to change other, bigger things.
Jacobs observes that if you do something diﬀerently, and you do it enough
times, it will change your mind. “If you force yourself to smile, you trick your
brain and then you start to become happier,” he says. Jacobs has tried this “act
as if” approach with everything from changing his posture to behaving as if he
were more condent than he is. When he nds that he’s doubting himself on a
project, he asks, What would an optimistic, condent person do? at person

<!-- PAGE 208 -->
## Source Page 208
would probably cast aside those doubts and forge ahead, so Jacobs tries to do
likewise.
IF YOU WANT to move from small behavior changes to bigger ones, now you
may need a particular type of question to help bring about that change—
namely, a “questolution.” Admittedly, it is a clunky name. But the idea is
sound: Take a resolution and reframe it as a question.
Why do that? Because questions can be more motivating than statements. A
study at the University of Illinois found that when people were trying to
motivate themselves to do something, they had better results asking (Will I do
X? How can I do X?) as opposed to declaring (I will do X!). Other studies have
found similar results: Questions do a better job than statements in terms of
encouraging behavioral change. So, rather than stating, “I will drink more
water, starting now!”, try asking yourself How might I get myself to drink more
water?
Why is the questolution more eﬀective? First oﬀ, questions tend to be more
mentally engaging than statements. ey invite you (or even challenge you) to
think about potential solutions, which gets your brain working right away on a
problem. e question is like a puzzle; you almost can’t help going to work on
it. And as we saw in chapter 5, our unconscious mind sometimes works on
unanswered questions when we’re sleeping or out on a walk. Ask yourself a
questolution and you may nd behavior-change epiphanies coming to you in
the shower (What if I get myself a cool water bottle that I’ ll carry everywhere?
What if I give myself a treat every time I down a glass of water?).
Questions are also less intimidating than resolutions. ey put less pressure
on you. Some people might feel they need pressure to get themselves to act,
but the self-imposed pressure of resolutions rarely produces immediate results
—and this, in turn, can cause us to quickly abandon those resolutions. A
question, on the other hand, is more forgiving and oﬀers more leeway. It
doesn’t necessarily have to be answered right away, or denitively: We can be
working on the question, taking steps, making progress toward an answer.
Plus, questions tend to be more “shareable” than statements. No one really
wants to hear your declarations of the wonderful things you’re promising to
do. But when you share a question with others (I wonder how I could do a

<!-- PAGE 209 -->
## Source Page 209
better job of X, or How might I improve Y?), it invites people to think about that
question themselves—and they may end up helping you to arrive at an answer.
Finally, resolutions are just too tied to New Year’s; questolutions can be done
anytime because . . . well, Why do people believe they can only make changes one
time a year? What if we start making changes now? How might we begin to do
that?
Can questioning help me to pursue my passion?
Moving up the ladder now from everyday habit changes to something more
substantial, let’s consider the “passion” question. Is this too much to ask of
questioning—to help you gure out what you’re meant to do in life?
It may be, if we’re expecting that merely asking a few questions will yield a
denitive answer. What questioning can do is help bring to the surface some
deep interests and buried talents—which can point you toward possible
activities, endeavors, or careers to think more about. Here’s a three-part
questioning approach to try.
Start by asking yourself, What are my signature strengths? e renowned
psychologist Martin Seligman says you can identify these qualities by thinking
and even writing about specic times when you’ve been at your best, then
inquiring more deeply about those successful episodes: What personal strengths
did I display when I was at my best? Did I show creativity? Good judgment?
Kindness? A more playful way of thinking about this is to ask: What are my
superpowers? e business consultant Keith Yamashita says this question can
help you to “unpack the combination of personality traits and aptitudes you
bring eﬀortlessly to any situation.”
Next, ask yourself questions about your natural interests. e idea is to
become “an anthropologist of your own life,” says coach and Essentialism
author Greg McKeown, who recommends asking yourself: When was I truly
happy and why? What activity or theme do I keep coming back to? and When do I
seem most like myself? is can include not only present activities, but past ones
as well—going back to childhood. e psychologist Eric Maisel recommends
asking, What did I enjoy doing at age ten? After drawing up a list of favorite
childhood activities, “see what still resonates with you today. And then it’s a

<!-- PAGE 210 -->
## Source Page 210
process of updating those loves. You may have loved something that doesn’t
even exist now, or doesn’t make sense in your life now—but you may be able to
nd a new version of that.” (Two more favorite questions for clarifying deep
interests: What makes me lose track of time?—meaning, pay attention to those
activities that put you in a state of “ow.” Also, What section do I always go to in
a bookstore?)
For part three, ask yourself about the ways in which you might contribute to
something larger than yourself. e psychologist and Grit author Angela
Duckworth has suggested that rather than just asking what you want to do
with your life, try asking: In what way do I wish the world were diﬀerent? What
problem can I help solve? “is puts the focus where it should be—on how you
can serve other people,” Duckworth added.
(On this same theme, I found a particular thought from Elie Wiesel, as
shared by Ariel Burger, to be moving and inspiring. Wiesel would often urge
his students to go out into the world and make a positive change; students, in
turn, would ask, Who should I help? What should I do? And Wiesel would tell
them to look around for someone in the world, or in their own community, or
maybe even in their own family, who was in need of assistance. As Wiesel put
it, beautifully, “Look for the outstretched hand.”)
QUESTIONING NOT ONLY can help you identify a passion, it can also
bolster the courage needed to pursue it. One of my favorite courage-building
questions is one you’ll no doubt recognize—it has been used not only by
coaches and psychologists, but also by parents everywhere, encouraging their
children to try something: What is the worst that could happen?
at may seem like a negative question because it evokes worst-case
imaginings, but our vague, exaggerated fears of the unknown often diminish
when subjected to rational thought. To make this question even more eﬀective,
try pairing it with a more positive follow-up question: And how would I recover
from that?
e author and entrepreneur Jonathan Fields recommends using these two
questions before embarking on a scary challenge. If you visualize what would
actually happen if you failed—and what you’d likely have to do to pick up the
pieces from that failure—this can help you realize that, as Fields says, “failure

<!-- PAGE 211 -->
## Source Page 211
in any endeavor is rarely absolute. ere is a way back from almost anything,
and once you acknowledge that, you can proceed with more condence.”
While you’re envisioning the possibility of failure, be sure to consider the
opposite, as well, by asking: What if I succeed—what would that look like? Fields
points out that this question is important because it can help counter our
natural bias toward negativity. He recommends visualizing, in detail, what
would be likely to happen in a best-case scenario. e reality may not live up
to that, but that vision can provide an incentive strong enough to encourage
taking a risk.
How will you ﬁnd your beautiful question?
When Doug Rauch came to the end of a successful career as the president of
the Trader Joe’s supermarket chain, he could have just retired and played golf.
But Rauch was hungry for something more. “I started looking at lots of
challenges but I kept coming back to the idea of ‘go with what you know,’ ”
Rauch recalls. “And what I know is food.” Something else he knew: “ere are
fty million Americans who are hungry.”
As Rauch began to think about this problem, he cycled through various
Whys, What Ifs, and Hows (he was no novice when it came to questioning—
he had practiced and honed the art at Trader Joe’s based on his belief that
“questioning is the heart of innovation”).
He started with Why do we have a situation in this country wherein one in six
people is hungry?
As he learned that almost half of the food produced in this country is never
consumed, Rauch found himself considering another question: Why does so
much good food end up in landlls?
What came next was a nice little bit of connective inquiry on Rauch’s part:
“You start to put the pieces together, then you think, What if you could use one
problem to solve the other?”
Being a supermarket guy, he knew one place where there was plenty of food
—on store shelves—which led to How do we get that food from the supermarket
to the food desert?

<!-- PAGE 212 -->
## Source Page 212
Rauch eventually came up with a potential solution: Create an operation
that would buy unused food at deep discounts from the supermarkets and
from other producers, repackage it as convenient and nutritious take-home
meals, and oﬀer it for pennies on the dollar in a large, indoor farmers’ market
in Boston.
Rauch wanted the food market to capture some of the allure of Trader Joe’s,
the sense that you’re getting quality food at a bargain price. Why not give the
food away? Rauch worked through that question and decided it’s not
sustainable, businesswise, nor is it what people necessarily want. Many tend to
eye food giveaways with suspicion; but all people like to think they’re getting a
deal. is realization sparked one of Rauch’s hypothetical questions: What if we
oﬀer a bargain instead of a handout?
Why not us?
When Russell Wilson was young, his father challenged him with a question. “My dad used to say, ‘Why not you, son? Why not you become a pro
football quarterback, and win a Super Bowl?’ ” The question stayed with Wilson as he worked his way up to becoming the quarterback of the
Seattle Seahawks. Then, in 2013, Wilson shared that inspirational question with his teammates. “We had a team meeting, and I told the players
that story about my father and said to them, Why not us? Why can’t we be there in the Super Bowl?” In February 2014, the Seahawks made it to
the big game—and Wilson became only the second Black quarterback in NFL history to win a Super Bowl. Ever since, Wilson has encouraged
young people to adopt a “why not me?” attitude. As he explains, “I think the reality is, that’s the fundamental question we all have to ask ourselves
at some point.”
Within a few years, he turned the idea into a reality. And Rauch’s story oﬀers
a good example of how one can come at the challenge of nding a beautiful
question to pursue. Rauch was bold and ambitious in staking out his question
—he took on one of the biggest, thorniest issues around. He did so at a stage
of life when, according to conventional wisdom, we should be pulling back
from the hard and the new, to settle into a life more comfortable and familiar.
In seeking out his question, he looked around to see what was needed in the
world. But he looked inside himself as well—to inquire about what he was
good at and how he might apply those skills in a fresh and meaningful way.
WE ALL HAVE goals and plans. But do you have one big, ambitious,
beautiful question that you’re pursuing? You should.

<!-- PAGE 213 -->
## Source Page 213
A beautiful question can be propulsive. Articulating a personal challenge in
the form of a question allows you to be bold and adventurous because anyone
can question anything. You don’t have to be a recognized expert; you just have
to be willing to say, I’m going to venture forth in the world with my question and
see what I nd.
As you do this, you’re in a strong position to build ideas and attract support.
Because, whereas people are more likely to ignore or challenge you when you
come at them with answers, they almost can’t resist advising or helping you to
answer a great question. All of this helps to build momentum. Questions (the
right ones, anyway) are good at generating momentum, which is why change-
makers so often use them as a starting point.
You may wonder, How do I gure out the right one for me?
To some degree the question chooses you. It’s the one that resonates with
you for some reason only you understand. What will make it a beautiful
question for you, and one worth staying with, is the passion you feel for it.
Look for a question that is “ambitious yet actionable”—or, as the physicist
Edward Witten puts it, a question that’s hard enough to be interesting, but
realistic enough that you have some hope of answering it. (Not that you have
to nd an answer to all beautiful questions; the string theorist Witten, for
instance, has never fully answered his biggest questions about the nature of the
universe, but he told me that the pursuit of those questions has led him to
many other interesting discoveries along the way.)
As some of the stories in this book have shown, people nd meaningful
questions in many ways and in various places. You can happen upon a great
question by an unfortunate accident, as Van Phillips did. Or, as with Edwin
Land, the question may be handed to you as an unexpected gift from an
inquisitive child. Or the question can spring from trying to come to terms
with a mundane problem: paying the rent, getting out of bed in the morning.
An interesting thing about beautiful questions is that you may not have to
search very far for them. ey’re often right in front of you—in your local
community, your company, or maybe in the palm of your hand. e trick is to
be able to see them, which may require stepping back, shifting perspective,
exercising your Carlin-esque powers of vuja de.

<!-- PAGE 214 -->
## Source Page 214
You can also nd beautiful questions outside of your familiar environs. Gary
White’s ongoing eﬀort to answer big questions about water, via his nonprot
group Water.org, all started when, as a student from the Midwest, he took a
trip to Guatemala and saw that many people lacked access to clean water. “It
struck me that here I was, just a short plane ride from the U.S., with all we
have—and here were these kids walking through sewage to collect
contaminated water that could kill them. So I just couldn’t help asking, Why
do so many lack this really basic thing that the rest of us take for granted?” Once
that question formed in his head, White was hooked.
ere’s no shortage in today’s world of wicked problems wrapped around
beautiful questions—meaning that somewhere deep inside that thorny issue,
embedded at the core, lies an undiscovered question of great value. If those
questions can be brought to the surface, we may be able to see the essence of
the problem more clearly.
ink of a complex social issue—questioners are likely hard at work
reframing it. Health care, hunger, protecting the environment, providing better
care for the aging—all of these issues and many more cry out for new and
better approaches that may only come to light via better questioning. en,
too, there is education, which is at the center of the questioning conversation.
ink of the fundamental questions that need asking, by teachers, students, by
education innovators such as the Right Question Institute, but also by parents
—because we know that parents who take the time and trouble to inquire How
can I encourage questioning in my child? are more likely to raise inquisitive kids
who grow up to be resourceful, problem-solving adults. at makes it a
beautiful question worth pursuing.
ON THE OTHER hand, maybe your beautiful question will focus on creating
a more fullled, more curious, more interesting you. When I asked Paul
Bennett, a creative director at the design rm IDEO, to share his own,
personal beautiful question, he responded, “e question I constantly ask
myself is How do I stay inspired?”
Bennett feels it’s part of his job to do so: “As creative chief of six hundred
people, you need to keep them inspired, but I can only do that if I keep myself
inspired.” He has trained himself to constantly notice and appreciate the

<!-- PAGE 215 -->
## Source Page 215
inspiration that’s all around. “You can’t do it all the time, but there are
moments in my day when I’ll say, ‘Stop—take a snapshot of this moment with
your mind, remember this.’ ” Bennett culls all of these bits and shares the best
of them with the people at IDEO, or with a larger audience in his writing for
blogs and publications.
What if I blend history with hip-hop?
The actor and playwright Lin-Manuel Miranda found his beautiful question waiting for him in a bookstore, within a book that he bought just to
have something to read on vacation. But as Miranda dug into Ron Chernow’s biography of the founding father Alexander Hamilton, something
clicked for him. Miranda absorbed the story of the immigrant Hamilton and, as he explained later, saw a larger story about immigration in
America (Miranda’s own father had arrived as an immigrant). And in the character of Hamilton—a rebel, a proliﬁc writer, and a man prone to
feuds—Miranda found parallels to modern-day rappers like Tupac Shakur. Before long, Miranda and his collaborative team were writing hip-hop
songs about Hamilton, and the songs eventually led to a Broadway musical that became a cultural phenomenon. Like so many great works of art,
Miranda’s Hamilton wrestles with deep questions throughout, and it even begins with a question—one that wonders how a bastard, an orphan,
a poor immigrant from the Caribbean, somehow managed to become one of the most important and inﬂuential ﬁgures in American history.
For many of us, the beautiful question that calls to us is some variation of
what Bennett is talking about: How do we continually nd inspiration so that we
can inspire others?
at question must be asked and answered afresh, over and over. ere is no
denitive answer, at least not for the creative individual who wants to keep
growing, improving, innovating.
Keep yourself away from the answers, but alive in the middle of the question—
this is the warning scrawled on the wall in the room where the acclaimed Irish
novelist Colum McCann does his writing. I asked McCann what he meant by
that line, and he wrote back, “We must embrace the notion that answers are in
fact quite boring. e Irish are especially good or infuriating in this respect.
We answer questions with questions. But in my opinion that’s a good place to
be. A little perplexed by the perplexity of life.”
It’s interesting that the beautiful questions about work—Why do the work we
do? What if we could take it to a diﬀerent place and another level? How, exactly,
might we do that?—persist even for many of those who’ve “made it.” I was
intrigued to see a New York Times interview with the lm actor Jake Gyllenhaal
in which he was asked about taking a detour from lms to tackle a demanding
role in a live-theater production. Gyllenhaal indicated that his previous run of

<!-- PAGE 216 -->
## Source Page 216
starring roles in big lms—for all the success it had brought—had somehow
failed to answer some deeper question for him. “I wasn’t really listening to
myself about the project I wanted to do,” he said. “I had to gure out what
kind of actor I wanted to be and feel condent going for that.”
Gyllenhaal also said that it was hard for young lm actors to do this kind of
introspection because “asking questions isn’t always a welcome thing in
Hollywood, where everyone seems like they know what they’re doing.” e
actor and director Ben Aﬄeck seems to have had a similar questioning
moment as he was embarking on his award-winning lm Argo. He’d already
directed two lms; he’d proven himself, up to a point. “After that,” Aﬄeck told
an interviewer, “the question became ‘Okay, you can do it. Now what do you
want to say?’ ”
at, right there, is a beautiful question for the ages: What do you want to
say? Why does it need to be said? What if you could say it in a way that has never
before been done? How might you do that?
WHEN YOU FIND your beautiful question, stay with it. If it’s a question
worth pursuing, it will likely also be confounding, frustrating, exhausting. If
you nd yourself stuck, follow the advice of the Acumen Fund’s Jacqueline
Novogratz: “Just try to get to the next question.” Break your big question into
smaller ones and work on those. Keep cycling through Whys, What Ifs, and
Hows, subjecting everything—even your being stuck—to a fresh set of queries.
Don’t be afraid to change your question—even to ratchet it down a notch.
You may also wish to expand it, broaden it, or possibly add pieces onto it,
turning it into a compound question (which can be a clunky yet beautiful
thing).
My own question started more than a decade ago as How can I write a book
that looks at something as familiar as questioning in a new way? But it turned out
writing a book was just a rst step on this particular journey of inquiry. e
book led to other things—such as visiting various types of organizations to
proselytize and share questioning tips and techniques. Somewhere along the
line I became focused on education—spending time with teachers, exploring
ways to encourage student questioning in all sorts of classrooms, whether in
urban areas or in rural Appalachia—because I think so much is riding upon

<!-- PAGE 217 -->
## Source Page 217
our ability, as a society, to develop the questioners, critical thinkers, and
innovators that the world will desperately need in days ahead. So now, my
beautiful question is something like this: How might I spread the word about
questioning, in as many ways and to as many receptive audiences as possible, but
with a particular focus on education? at’s a question I expect to be “living
with” for a good long time.
As you live with your own question, be sure to tell others about it; put it out
there on social media, share it any way you can. You’ll be amazed at how
people tend to support and help someone pursuing a beautiful question. (If
you want to share your question with the readers of this book, there is a page
on the book’s website with beautiful questions from readers:
amorebeautifulquestion.com/whats-your-beautiful-question).
Take your question for walks in the woods and take it to a museum; it will
tend to come alive in such places. Create the time and space for inspiration,
which, as Van Phillips observes, comes in unexpected waves. As an innovator,
“you’re like a surfer waiting patiently for that wave to come in,” says Phillips
(yes, he surfs, as well as runs, on that foot he made). You don’t know when the
wave will come in—when those unpredictable connections in the nether
regions of the brain will happen—but you must prepare and be ready for it. If
you haven’t suﬃciently thought about your question—if you haven’t even
asked it—the connections are unlikely to happen and the wave may never
materialize.
You may discover, as many questioners do when they begin to burrow into a
problem, that there is much more to know than you could have imagined at
the outset. Don’t be put oﬀ by learning how much you don’t know. at
darkness was always out there, surrounding you; you just had no idea how vast
it was until you began probing with your question ashlight. Questioners learn
to love that great unknown—it’s the land of opportunity, in terms of creativity
and innovation. e author Stuart Firestein thinks we should all learn to see it
likewise and oﬀers up this beautiful question: What if we cultivated ignorance
instead of fearing it?
If we did that, we would need some cultivating tools—including one, in
particular, that could help us dig, uncover, tend, and grow.

<!-- PAGE 218 -->

What if it turned out that tool had been right there in our back pocket, ever

particularly helpful in pulling together complex information about articial

I want to thank all the people who granted interviews. I’ve been a
magazine/newspaper journalist and I’ve been a book author and there is an
important diﬀerence that manifests itself during the research process: When
you’re calling from the New York Times or Wired magazine, people tend to
respond because they want the publicity. But when you call and say, “I’m
writing a book”—well, then you are dependent on the kindness of strangers.
ere’s not much in it for the interviewee; they have no idea if the book will
ever get written or if anyone will read it once it comes out. So I think they do
the interview primarily because they have a genuine interest in the idea you’re
pursuing, and they want to be of help.
With that in mind, I’m grateful to all who took the time to talk about
questioning, including Dr. Ken Heilman, Gretchen Rubin, Irene Au, John

Chapter 4: Asking Why (to Understand)
Edwin Land was a brilliant inventor In recounting the Polaroid story, I relied on a number of sources,
starting with Life magazine’s cover story “A Genius and His Camera,” October 27, 1972. In the article,
Land explains, in his own words, how his daughter asked him why she couldn’t see the photograph right
away. A good roundup on the Polaroid story also appears in Harry McCracken, “Polaroid SX-70: e Art
and Science of the Nearly Impossible,” Technologizer, June 8, 2011,
technologizer.com/2011/06/08/polaroid/. Another good source was “Polaroid Corporation,”
International Directory of Company Histories, vol. 93 (Detroit, MI: St. James Press, 2008). I rst learned
about the Polaroid question story from Mike Brewster, “e Great Innovators: Instant Photos, Lasting
Fame,” Business Week, June 29, 2004. Several good books have been written about Land and Polaroid,
including Victor K. McElheny, Insisting on the Impossible: e Life of Edwin Land (New York: Basic
Books, 1999). But I am particularly indebted to Christopher Bonanos’s book Instant: e Story of Polaroid
(Princeton, NJ: Princeton Architectural Press, 2012). See also Bonanos, “e Man Who Inspired Jobs,”
New York Times, October 7, 2011. Bonanos is slightly skeptical about the veracity of the “daughter’s
question” story, whether Land embellished his own tale; nevertheless, I choose to accept Land’s story as
truth—if not a literal one, then a larger one.

A Note on the Author
Warren Berger is the author of multiple books, including e Book of Beautiful
Questions and Glimmer. A frequent public speaker, he has also appeared on
NBC’s Today show, ABC World News Tonight, CNN, and NPR’s All ings
Considered. He lives with his wife Laura E. Kelly in Westchester, New York.

<!-- PAGE 252 -->
## Source Page 252
Also available from Warren Berger
e Book of Beautiful Questions
From the bestselling author of A More Beautiful Question, hundreds of questions that harness the
magic of inquiry to tackle challenges we all face—at work, in our relationships, and beyond.
When we’re confronted with almost any demanding situation, the act of questioning can help guide us to
smart decisions in the face of uncertainty. But the questions must be the right ones; the ones that cut to
the heart of complexity or enable us to see an old problem in a fresh way.
In e Book of Beautiful Questions, expert “questionologist” Warren Berger shares illuminating stories and
compelling research on the power of inquiry. Drawing from the insights and expertise of psychologists,
innovators, eﬀective leaders, and some of the world’s foremost creative thinkers, he presents the essential
questions readers need to make the best choices when it truly counts, with a particular focus in four key
areas: decision-making, creativity, leadership, and relationships.
e powerful questions in this book can help you:
Identify opportunities in your career or industry
Generate fresh ideas in business or in your own creative pursuits
Check your biases so you can make better judgments and decisions
Do a better job of communicating and connecting with the people around you
oughtful, provocative, and actionable, these beautiful questions can be applied immediately to bring
about change in your work or your everyday life.
